With mDNS off, two seats on one box reach each other by going out to a public circuit relay and back. **Which makes this a property of the harness before it is a defect in the client**, and the harness says so itself: `-NoMdns` is on by default because *“two machines on different subnets cannot find each other by multicast”*, and leaving mDNS on for the local seats made them *“a clique that finds itself in a second while the far ones come the long way”*. The measurement is therefore honest about strangers and pessimistic about neighbours: in production mDNS is on and two players on one LAN would be instant, while ten strangers on ten home connections would pay exactly this. **The founder's admission timeline shows the same thing from the other side** — seats at 3.4, 7.1, 30.6, then a 44-second gap, then six seats between 74.9 and 86.1, then a 52-second gap and the tenth at 138.1. Bursts and gaps, not a steady handshake rate: relay circuits coming up in clumps. **One reading was wrong on the way here and is corrected in place.** *“Nine `asking to join` prints and the guard fired zero times, so the join never produced a Formation”* is right about the fact and wrong about the cause: `asking to join` is printed by the harness at `main.rs:470` **before** the command reaches the node loop, and the guard did not fire because there was no Formation to guard — the request had nowhere to go, because there was no connection to the founder yet. The join RPC was never the bottleneck. **What is not established** is why a relayed circuit to a peer on the same machine takes ninety-seven seconds when another takes two, on the same host in the same run. That is the same unexplained variance as `S1-AH`'s 56-second cross-network dial and it belongs with it. **FIXED, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS THE ONE THIS ROW ASKED FOR.** The advert carries `founder_peer_id` and **no address**, and `JoinTable` called `send_request` immediately — so the dial could only succeed if the swarm already held an address for that peer id. Nothing put one there deliberately: `get_closest_peers` appeared nowhere in `run.rs`, and the founder's addresses arrived only as a by-product of the sixty-second lobby crawl. It was a lottery on whether the address happened to be in the routing table at the moment of the join. **A targeted lookup at join time settled it.** Founder-connection times, nine seats, before and after: **2.2 / 26.9 / 48.4 / 66.9 / 70.0 / 74.8 / 75.0 / 77.7 / 97.5** against **1.5 / 5.2 / 7.2 / 7.6 / 7.7 / 9.0 / 25.2 / 53.5 / 66.9**. Median **70 → 7.7 seconds**, six of nine now under ten where one was before, and connection-to-seat down to 0.0–2.0 s on every seat. No wire change and no new field in the advert: one query whose only purpose is to learn where a peer id lives. **And the bottleneck moved, which is worth saying rather than celebrating a median.** `split163641-10` finished forming at 97 s and then played **four** hands in the remaining five hundred seconds — opening them at 108.3, 170.1, 266.8 and 307.9 s and stalling after the fourth. The accounting still balances at zero unexplained on every node and the drop counts are the lowest yet measured, with `n0` and `far-n4` at **zero**. So what is slow now is the hand loop, not formation, and that is a different question from the one this row answers. | OPEN: `docs/NETWORK_STACK.md` §4, `src/net/run.rs` — **measured, not fixed**: formation time is the founder connection and nothing else; the join RPC and the Tox group are both fast, and the relayed-even-locally path is the harness's deliberate choice |
| S1-AO | **A full table cannot certify a timeout, so one seat missing one message freezes the table for ever. Found by asking why `split163641-10` played four hands in ten minutes.** Hand #4 opened on all ten seats, began on all ten, and never ended. Nine seats stopped at *“your turn — 100 to call”*; the tenth, `far-n0` = seat 2, stopped one step earlier at *“the deck is shuffled and sealed”* — it never learned it was on the clock. **The mechanism the protocol has for exactly this worked.** Each of the nine ran its clock out on seat 2 and the tally climbed 1/9, 2/9 … to **9/9 agree at 376.0 s**. The table was unanimous, on one digest, `@a14b83d8`. **And then it could not say so.** From the moment the ninth vote landed, every seat logged `hand: TooLong("the payload is over its cap")` once per attempt for the remaining four minutes of the run. **The arithmetic, measured rather than argued.** A sealed `TIMEOUT_VOTE` is **266 B**. `TimeoutCert::votes` carries *“the whole signed events and not just their signatures”*, `MAX_SEATS - 1` of them, and it is a plain `Vec<Vec<u8>>` with `#[n(1)]` — **no `minicbor::bytes`**, unlike `key`, `proof`, `deck`, `apk` and `token`, every other opaque byte field in the same file — so each vote encodes as a CBOR array of integers rather than a byte string and very nearly doubles. Nine votes plus the digest come to **4 699 B** at their widest against `TIMEOUT_CERT_CAP` = **4 096**. **And the real figure is sharper than the worst case, which is what makes this so easy to miss.** A separate reading rebuilt the structs against minicbor 2.3.0 with production-shaped values: one sealed vote is **240 B**, the certificate body is **3 647 B at eight voters** — comfortably inside the cap — and **4 100 B at nine**. **The margin between a table that works and a table that freezes for ever was four bytes.** Nine voters happens only at a full ten-seat table, which is why no earlier run reached it. **Nothing caught it because nothing ever built a large one.** `tests/timeout_certificate.rs` is 1 100 lines and every case in it opens **three** seats — its own comment says *“at three seats the voter set is two”* — so the largest certificate this repo had ever encoded carried **two** votes against a documented maximum of nine. The cap and the doc beside it were never compared. **FIXED, narrowly and on purpose.** `TIMEOUT_CERT_CAP` is **8 192**: the next power of two above the measured worst case, 74 % headroom. `tests/timeout_certificate_at_a_full_table.rs` pins the arithmetic at `MAX_SEATS - 1` so it cannot drift under again, and asks the same question of `HAND_COMPLETE_CAP`, whose doc likewise describes a maximum nobody had built — that one **fits**, measured, not reasoned. Full suite green, 791 tests. **What is deliberately NOT fixed, and it is the owner's.** Annotating `votes` with `minicbor::bytes` would nearly halve the certificate — 4 699 B to roughly 2 460 — which is worth having for a reason beyond tidiness: this message is sent precisely when the network is already struggling, and a smaller body is fewer fragments to lose. But it changes the bytes on the wire, hence the event hash, hence everything chained from it. That is a protocol decision, not a bug fix, and it belongs beside `S1-AE`'s wire fields. **And the severity is worth stating plainly.** This is the worst failure mode the protocol has, because to every honest seat a frozen table is indistinguishable from a table that is simply over. No seat is wrong, no seat is malicious, the vote is unanimous, and the game stops. It could only ever happen at nine or ten seats, which is why every run before this one missed it. | OWNER: `src/table/hand.rs` (`TIMEOUT_CERT_CAP`), `src/table/handwire.rs` (`TimeoutCert::votes` — **encoding is the owner's call**), `tests/timeout_certificate_at_a_full_table.rs` |
| S1-AP | **Three things about how this client reports a dead hand, all found while reading `split163641-10`, and each cost a whole reading. Two are fixed; one is the owner's.** **(a) The abort sentence named the wrong clock. FIXED.** `past_deadline` is an OR of two budgets: the hand's own, `RATED_HAND_DEADLINE_MS` = 3 300 000 for a rated ten-seat table, and the stage's, `crypto_step_timeout_ms` = 30 000, which applies to every non-betting phase. `abort_now(Abort::Deadline)` carries no reason, so both printed *“the hand ran out of time”*. `n0` opened hand 1 at 108.3 s and aborted at 169.3 s — sixty seconds. Nothing in a three-minute-old process consumed fifty-five minutes, so the clock that fired was the stage's and the sentence pointed at the other one. `Hand::expired_budget` now names which, with the elapsed time, the budget and the seats still owed. **(b) The void was printed several times per seat. FIXED.** `aborted()` is a standing property of the phase, not an event, and that block had no once-flag while `deck_reported`, `cards_reported` and `turn_reported` all do — so every further event accepted before `next_hand_at` replaced the hand reprinted it. `far-n3` four times, `far-n1` four, `far-n0` three. **Anyone counting seats from a grep over-counts them**, in the one message that says a hand died — and this row's own earlier arithmetic did exactly that. **(c) The 53-second void cascade is not the wire, and the fix for it is a rules change this implementation may not make.** The abort is **one NGC group broadcast** and it arrives promptly; `on_hand_abort` returns nothing, so there is no relayed re-broadcast. What takes 53 s is that every receiver **refuses it** — `Failed::NotYet`, parked in `self.early` — until **its own** `stage_at_ms + 30 s` has passed. Ten independent clocks, not latency. The two seats already past their own stage deadline when `n0` broadcast accepted it within a second; the rest took 6 to 26 s, and hand #1's *“has begun”* was itself spread over 42.7 s, which is most of the total. **The gate is not a defect.** It is what stops one peer unilaterally killing a hand the others are happily playing: a bare `cause = 1` abort names nobody and carries no certificate, so a receiver that trusted it on sight would let any seat end any hand. Collapsing the remaining stage timeout on sight of a signed abort — the obvious fix — weakens exactly that protection, and counting N distinct signed aborts before releasing the hold is a quorum rule, which is a wire decision. **So it is filed rather than fixed, beside `S1-AE`.** **And one thing that was checked and must not be touched.** The mental shuffle's ~1.3 s per seat is **genuinely serial**: `accept_step` verifies against `self.decks.last()`, `remask_card` re-encrypts the predecessor's actual ciphertexts, and the Fiat-Shamir challenge is a hash over **both** decks — so seat *k* has no object to work on until seat *k-1* has emitted one, and the security property rests on it. Ten dependent hops is the construction, not an implementation choice. Only the per-hop constant is open. | OWNER: `src/table/hand.rs` (`expired_budget`), `src/net/run.rs` (`abort_reported`), `src/mental_poker/shuffle.rs` (**do not touch**) — **(c) is open and is the owner's** |
| S1-AQ | **A timeout certificate needs a unanimous vote from a set that includes peers who have already ended the hand, so at a full table it can be unreachable — and the denominator cannot shrink without first shrinking. Found by verifying `S1-AO`'s fix, which unmasked it.** **The measurement, `split173908-10`, ten seats over two machines.** `S1-AO`'s fix works: `TooLong` went from 218 occurrences to **zero** and all ten seats finished a hand where none had. Then hand #4 stopped anyway. Seat 6 (`far-n2`) ended it locally at 188.3 s. The other seats' clocks ran out on seat 5 and they began voting at 224.4 s, reaching **8/9 at 225.5 s** — where it stayed for the remaining 375 seconds of the run. Eight nodes cast that vote 32 to 36 times each; `far-n2` cast it **zero** times, because it had left hand #4 thirty-six seconds before the first vote was cast. Meanwhile seats 5 and 6 voided hand #4 on their own clocks and ran on to hand #10, opening five hands the other eight never joined. The table forked and neither half could recover. **The rule, in the code, and it is a closed circle.** `voters(subject)` is `mine.dealt_in` minus the subject minus `self.certified`; `certify_if_unanimous` refuses while `held < voters.len()`, so **every** live voter must vote. The only term that can shrink the denominator is `certified` — and a seat only enters `certified` by being certified out, which needs the unanimity that the denominator is blocking. **You cannot shrink the set without first shrinking the set.** A peer that has terminated the hand stays in `dealt_in`, stays a required voter, and can never vote again. **Why this could not have shown up before.** It needs a seat to leave the hand *before* the others start voting, which needs the two halves of the table to be in different stages, which needs enough seats for a stage to spread — the whole three-seat test suite has no room for it. And until `S1-AO` was fixed, the certificate never got far enough for the quorum rule to be the binding constraint. **And it is not a partition.** `n0` at 212.4 s reports every one of the nine reachable at 36 to 326 ms, `group 9 seen/9 confirmed/9 wanted`, `tox friends up 9`. The seats disagree about the hand, not about the network — which is what makes it a rules question rather than a transport one. **Open, and the owner's, because every repair is a rules change.** Deriving the denominator from who is still in the hand makes it per-receiver, which is D-012's forbidden shape and `Q-10`'s subject exactly. A threshold instead of unanimity is a quorum rule and a wire decision. Letting a departing seat publish a terminal that removes it from `dealt_in` is `S1-O`'s monotone-roster question from the other end. This row does not choose; it records that the three are one question and that a full table is where it stops being theoretical. **One thing measured and worth separating out: the seats were not passive.** `n0` played `autoplay: Call` at 200.8 s and voted 32 times. The self-fold answer `PROTOCOL.md` relies on — *“a seat that goes quiet in a betting stage is answered by its own client folding for it”* — never fired anywhere in the run (`the table acted for seat` appears **zero** times), but that is because no seat was quiet. The hand did not stall on a silent player. It stalled on a quorum that could not be completed.  **HALF FIXED, AND THE MEASUREMENT SHARPENS THE OTHER HALF INTO A DIFFERENT QUESTION.** **What was fixed.** Two expressions of one judgement disagreed. `past_deadline` returns `false` for a betting stage by construction — deliberately, because a player thinking is legitimate — while `past_stage_deadline`, which fires the `TIMEOUT_VOTE`, covers every stage. So a client could vote that a stage had timed out and, in the same breath, refuse a peer asserting the same thing; the abort sat in `self.early` for ever. The cause-1 gate is now `past_deadline \|\| long_past_stage` — **a union, never a replacement** (`past_stage_deadline` is `false` when `owed_type` is `None` and ignores the whole-hand budget, so a replacement would delete the 55-minute backstop and re-create the same deadlock elsewhere), and at **twice** the stage budget rather than once. **The factor of two is not caution, it is the whole safety argument.** An adversarial reading found the 1x version fatally exploitable: a player drawing dead simply stops acting, which manufactures `past_stage_deadline` on every receiver for free, and then broadcasts a bare abort — every seat accepts, the pot is destroyed and the staller is refunded. At 2x the certificate round at 1 % has a full stage-deadline of clear air to seal, force the fold and reset `stage_at_ms`, so the parked abort re-parks. `may_abandon` already used `long_past_stage` for exactly this reason (*“the better mechanism gets to go first”*); the two sides of one rule now agree. Also verified exhaustively: the cryptographic case is never loosened, because only `next_deadline_for`'s action branch can be shorter than `crypto_step_timeout_ms` and that branch is reachable only via `ACTION_GROUP`, which `stage_type` produces only at `Step::Acting`, where `crypto_stage()` is `false` by construction. Second half of the fix: the stall tick now calls `replay_early`, which had exactly one caller — the `Ok` arm of `hand_event!` — so a message parked on a **clock** was reconsidered only by an **event**. **Measured, `split182531-10`, ten seats over two machines: the fork is gone.** All ten nodes in lockstep — five hands opened, four finished, identically on every seat — against `split173908-10`'s 4-versus-10 split. Zero `TooLong`, zero aborts, zero stranded seats. **And the hand that did stall exposed a shape this row had wrong.** Hand #5 hung with all ten seats at *“your turn”* and nothing terminated it. The reason is not a seat that left. **Two distinct subjects each reached 8/9 and neither went further** — digests `@d7280ced` and `@d106bbd3` — and the votes were split across them: `far-n3` cast its votes about seats **0 and 5**, while the majority cast theirs about seats **1 and 7**. Two disjoint opinions about who was holding the hand up, so neither could ever be unanimous. **So unanimity requires the voters to agree not merely that somebody is late, but on who**, and any divergence in `waiting_for()` splits the vote so that no certificate can form. It happened **twice in one run**. **WITHDRAWN: *“and it is not the transport”* was read off a run that was not measuring the transport.** This row said every node reported zero ring-full refusals, zero unhandled packets and zero retransmits. Those zeroes are worthless. `src/tox/mod.rs` registers `tox_options_set_log_callback` **only** under `--features fault-harness`, so a binary built without it writes no toxcore line of any level — not merely no `DEBUG`. The build step added to `tools/table-run-split.ps1` earlier the same day omitted the feature: `split163641-10` carries 155 toxcore lines on `n0` alone, `split173908-10` and `split182531-10` carry **zero on every node**. The events were not absent; the instrument was. The harness now builds with the feature and prints which, and `-Quiet` has to be asked for. **What the same run does support, because it is counted in Rust rather than logged by toxcore, is the opposite conclusion.** The send side is badly behind: `far-n0` reports **404 messages queued**, `n0` **2 481 fragments refused of 6 804 offered** (36 %), `far-n1` 1 854 of 5 638 (33 %), while `n2` and `n4` never report being behind at all. And seats learn it is their turn **178 to 326 seconds** after the deck is sealed, on a link whose measured per-seat latency is 36 to 326 **milliseconds** — seat 0 at 4.5 s and seat 9 at 4.6 s against seat 1 at 178 s and seats 7 and 8 at 326 s. So the divergence that split the vote is downstream of a transport that is minutes behind, which is very nearly the reverse of what this row claimed. **And the refusals cannot yet be attributed**, which is its own defect: `Tox::send` returns the exact `Tox_Err_Group_Send_Custom_Packet` in `Failed::Api` and `flush` discarded it for a bare counter. Code 4 (`DISCONNECTED`) is decided inside `tox_group_send_custom_packet` before any peer is consulted; code 5 (`FAIL_SEND`) comes from the peer loop where `patches/0003` lives. The remedies have nothing in common. The counter now records which. *(An earlier draft of this row named `n0` as the odd one out and cited a `waiting for seat 8` line. Both were wrong: that line came from the `HAND_INIT` stage rather than the betting one, and the per-node counts behind it were of tally lines — votes **received** — rather than votes **cast**. `n0` voted about seat 1 with everybody else.)* **That reframes the remaining decision rather than answering it.** Widening `may_abandon` to originate a bare abort from a betting stage is the obvious way to stop the hang, and it is right in one of the two cases and wrong in the other. Where the table agrees who is late and one voter has merely gone, the certificate would have **forced that seat's fold and the hand would have continued to its rightful winner** — `certify` over an action stage calls `play.round.apply` and `after_action`, it does **not** end the hand — so voiding is a strict loss and it refunds whoever was losing. Where the table cannot agree who is late, the hand is genuinely unrecoverable and voiding is the honest terminus. The two are locally indistinguishable today. And because ordinary packet loss produces the second case routinely, widening `may_abandon` does not merely add a rare terminus: it makes voiding common, which is precisely the outcome a staller who is beaten would choose. **Both failure modes have one source — unanimity is unreachable under ordinary loss — and that is the thing to decide.** Until it is decided, neither `may_abandon` nor a threshold has been touched. | OWNER: `src/table/hand.rs` (`may_abandon`, `long_past_stage`, `past_stage_deadline`, the cause-1 gate) — **the decision this row ends on is the owner's**: whether unanimity being unreachable under ordinary loss is answered by widening `may_abandon` or by a threshold. Neither has been touched. *(This row had no sources column at all until 2026-09-06: an unescaped Rust or-operator in its own prose split it, and the table had read as four columns ever since.)* |
| S1-AR | **The table's fragment was sized to the largest packet toxcore's API will accept, which is nearly three times the largest one it carries whole — so every fragment was cut again underneath, at four times the cost. It is the root of the stalls `S1-AO` and `S1-AQ` were found chasing.** `fragment::TOX_PACKET` was **1 373**, `TOX_GROUP_MAX_CUSTOM_LOSSLESS_PACKET_LENGTH`. But `send_lossless_group_packet` sends a packet whole only while `length <= MAX_GC_PACKET_CHUNK_SIZE`, which is **500**. Above it the library fragments again, beneath a layer that has already fragmented, and the second cut compounds in two ways: **four message ids instead of one** (500 + 500 + 374 + a terminator, each taking a slot of that peer's 2 047-entry send ring), and **only the terminating chunk is acked** — `handle_gc_packet_fragment` acks on `frag_ret == 0` alone, so three quarters of every entry sit in the ring until a retransmission triggers the duplicate path, a minimum of three seconds and 5, 9, 17 or 33 on a lost one. **The arithmetic is the whole finding.** At a measured ten fragments a second that is forty ids per peer per second, and 2 047 slots fill in **51 seconds** — *seven seconds before* `GC_CONFIRMED_PEER_TIMEOUT` (58 s), which is the bound `patches/0003` explicitly relies on to release a peer that cannot accept. The bound arrives too late, and never at all if the peer acks intermittently. **CORRECTION: the evidence first cited for this mechanism was misread, and it does not support it.** This row said *“of 8 355 `create_array_entry` failures, 8 292 are type `0xf2`, `GP_FRAGMENT`”*. **`0xf2` is `GP_CUSTOM_PACKET`.** `GP_FRAGMENT` is **`0xef`** (`group_chats.h:94, 97`), and `0xef` appears **zero times in every run measured** — `split203824-10`, `split210848-10`, `split212806-10` and `split215300-10` alike. Those failures were ordinary poker packets, and the send-side ones carry `length: 302`, comfortably under the 500-byte boundary. Nothing in the failure logs shows the internal fragment path being taken at all, because only *failures* are logged and fragment entries did not fail. **What survives is the measurement, which was a controlled comparison.** `split203824-10` and `split210848-10` differ in `TOX_PACKET` and in nothing else, and the numbers below are what changed. **What does not survive is the claim that those particular counts prove why.** The mechanism — that a packet over `MAX_GC_PACKET_CHUNK_SIZE` is cut again into four message ids and only its terminating chunk acked — is read from `send_lossless_group_packet` and `handle_gc_packet_fragment`, and a nine-kilobyte `SHUFFLE_STEP` costs 7 x 4 = 28 ids at 1 373 against 19 x 1 at 500 by that reading. It is **derived from the source and not yet witnessed in a log**, and this row previously implied otherwise. **Separately and reliably**, `patches/0003` logged *“custom packet reached **8 of 9** confirmed peers”* **647 times** in `split203824-10`: one peer unable to accept, the whole table's message refused, re-queued and re-offered at one per 50 ms tick. That count is unambiguous — it is the patch's own line — and it is what made one peer's trouble the table's. **FIXED by the constant: `TOX_PACKET` is now 500**, the boundary at which nothing is cut twice. Smaller fragments are cheaper despite being more numerous: a nine-kilobyte `SHUFFLE_STEP` costs seven packets of four ids at 1 373 and nineteen packets of one id at 500 — 19 against 28, none of them on the ack-starved path. It is transport framing and not protocol, as `fragment::HEADER`'s own comment says: it sits under the signed envelope, both ends of one table run one build, and `tools/table-run-split.ps1` verifies that by SHA-256 before it measures anything. **MEASURED, and it is the largest single improvement of the day.** `split203824-10` and `split210848-10` differ in that constant and in nothing else: receive-side ring failures **19 768 → 2 072**; `Failed to add payload to send array` **800 → 3**; hand #3's sealed-to-your-turn across ten seats **5.8–8.5 s → 2.0–4.1 s**. A third run with `patches/0006` as well, `split212806-10`, reached **148** ring failures, **zero** partial sends, **zero** send-array failures, turn latency **1.5–3.7 s**, and **twelve hands opened and eleven finished identically on all ten seats** — against four hands and a forked table at the start of the day. **But the third run does not price `patches/0006`, and saying so matters.** That patch's own log line — *“peer N is confirmed but cannot be sent to; not waited for”* — fired **zero** times, because the run had **zero** re-handshakes where the previous one had three. Its branch was never entered. The step from `split210848-10` to `split212806-10` is the absence of the condition, not its repair, and `0006` remains **correct by reading and unexercised by measurement** until a run reproduces a peer mid-handshake. **And one claim withdrawn:** `patches/0003` is an **amplifier and not the initiating cause**. In `split182531-10` the first divergence happened at 255-281 s while `n0` had an empty queue and zero refusals until 377.5 s, and the earliest refusal anywhere in that run is 52 seconds after the table had already split. **One latent defect found in the same reading and deliberately left alone.** `group_connection.c:190` truncates the 64-bit `gconn->send_message_id` into a `uint16_t start_id` and lines 229 and 238 use it to *rewind* the stream. It is reachable only on a mid-sequence send-array overflow — the exact state the retry loop above holds at 20 Hz. `n0` reached roughly 25 000 ids per peer in one ten-minute run; at 65 536 the stream silently rewinds by up to 65 535 messages and is destroyed permanently. Not the cause of anything measured yet, and two more storms of this size would reach it. | FIXED: `src/table/fragment.rs` (`TOX_PACKET`), `patches/0003`, `vendor/c-toxcore/toxcore/group_connection.c:190` (**latent, untouched**) |
| S1-AS | **The send ring's own overflow guard cannot fire while the oldest unacked message sits at index 0, because its test underflows — so a full ring is not reported as full, it is reported as a message that will not fit.** Asked directly: why were the sends refused. **The guard, `group_connection.c` in `add_to_send_array`:** `if ((gconn->send_message_id % GCC_BUFFER_SIZE) == (uint16_t)(gconn->send_array_start - 1))`. `send_array_start` is a `uint16_t` (`group_common.h:104`). When it is **0**, `send_array_start - 1` promotes to `int`, gives `-1`, and the cast makes it **65 535**. The left side is a value modulo 2 048 and is therefore at most **2 047**. The equality is unreachable. So for as long as the oldest unacknowledged message occupies slot 0 — which is exactly the state a stalled peer holds — the ring can be completely full and the guard says nothing. Control passes to `create_array_entry`, which finds the slot occupied and fails there instead. **Measured, and the decomposition is what proves it.** `add_to_send_array` has exactly two failure paths, the guard and the entry. Across `split203824-10`, `split210848-10` and `split212806-10` the guard's own line, *“Send array overflow”*, appears **zero times in all three runs** — while `Failed to add payload to send array` appears **800**, **3** and **0**. Every refused send went down the second path. The ring was full and its overflow check never once said so. **It also corrects this session's own arithmetic, in the direction of the finding rather than against it.** `create_array_entry` is called from both `add_to_send_array` and `store_in_recv_array` and logged one sentence for both, so every count reported today was the sum of two unrelated faults — back-pressure and head-of-line blocking. Splitting it by the guard's zero: of `split203824-10`'s 19 768, **800 are send-side and 18 968 receive-side**, so the *“receive-side”* label was about 96 % right and the principle was wrong anyway. `patches/0007` now puts the side, the message id and the packet type in the message so the two can never be added together again. **Not fixed here, and deliberately.** Repairing the guard — comparing against `send_array_start` rather than one before it, or widening the arithmetic — changes when libtoxcore refuses a send, which is a behaviour change in the layer a hand rides. What it would do is turn a mislabelled failure into an honest one; it would not deliver a single extra message, because the ring really is full at that point. The remedy for a full ring is to stop filling it, which is what `S1-AR`'s fragment sizing did: 19 768 to 2 072 total, 800 to 3 on the send side. The guard is filed as an upstream defect with its consequence stated, and the honest statement is that it costs diagnosis rather than delivery. **What that costs concretely, learned on 2026-09-04 while using it as evidence: a count of zero `Send array overflow` lines does not prove the ring never filled**, because for one lap in 2048 — whenever `send_array_start` is 0 — the guard cannot fire and the ring is caught one slot later by `create_array_entry`'s *entry is not empty* instead. Anyone arguing from the ring's health must count **both** strings, and better still `Failed to add payload to send array`, which subsumes them. The argument that the ring stayed clean through 900 s at ten seats survives only because all three were counted and all three were zero. | FIXED: `vendor/c-toxcore/toxcore/group_connection.c` (`add_to_send_array`), `patches/0007` |
| S1-AT | **A seat's budget to act is a fixed 25 seconds that does not scale with the seat count, while the time to learn it is on the clock does. At ten seats delivery alone eats up to 19.7 of the 25, so honest players are certified out for a silence they had no chance to break — and the table sheds seats until it is fast enough for its own deadline.** **Measured, `split215300-10`, ten seats over two machines, 900 s.** Sealed-to-*your-turn*, every seat that logged both: hand #3 at ten seats **8.0 8.3 8.4 8.4 14.2 14.2 14.6 14.6 15.2 15.3**; hand #4 at ten seats **6.3 11.7 15.2 17.4 17.7 17.7 17.9 18.2 18.9 19.7**; hand #7 at seven seats 5.5 to 12.3; hand #8 and #9 at six seats **1.0 to 2.0**. The cost scales hard with the table and very nearly vanishes below seven. **And the budget it is spent against is `action_timeout_ms` (20 s) + `action_grace_ms` (5 s), a flat number**, while the *hand* budget is already derived from the seat count by `hand_deadline_min_ms(seats, …)`. The action budget is not. At ten seats a player has five seconds of the twenty-five left to think; at three it has twenty-three. **The consequence is visible as attrition, agreed by every peer rather than forked.** The table opened hand #1 with ten seats and hand #20 with three, narrowing at each step and each narrowing logged identically on all remaining nodes: `[0..9]` → `[0,1,3,4,5,6,7,8,9]` → `[0,1,3,4,5,6,7,9]` → `[0,1,3,4,5,6,9]` → `[0,1,3,5,6,9]` → `[1,3,5,6]` → `[1,3,6]`. Twenty-two readmissions fired — the `S1-O` door works — and did not keep up with the certifying. **The table shrank until the lag fitted the budget**, and at three seats it did: 1 to 2 seconds. **Three things this run rules out, and one of them was this session's own framing.** Send-side blocking: four send-ring failures in 900 s across ten nodes, zero `Send array overflow`, zero partial sends. A fork: hand #8's genesis is byte-identical on every node that opened it. And **receive-ring pressure, which this session had called *“the one remaining brake”* — it is not**, because it does not correlate with progress at all: `n3` had **zero** failures and opened **20** hands, `far-n1` had **1 252** and opened **16**, `far-n0` had 228 and opened **8**. Of `far-n1`'s failing ids 534 failed once and 359 twice, which is the shape of retransmitted duplicates of already-buffered messages being logged as errors rather than of head-of-line blocking. **ANSWERED, AND THE DIAGNOSIS IN THIS ROW WAS A SYMPTOM.** The 19.7 seconds was not delivery scaling with the seat count. It was the certificate churn: **443 certificates refused table-wide**, every one emitted by a seat that had been certified out and was still voting for itself. That is `S1-AU`, and it is one missing guard on the emit side of `vote_on_timeouts`. **Measured across the fix, both runs 900 s at ten seats over two machines** — `split215300-10` against `split231054-10`: refused certificates **443 → 0**; roster narrowings **52 → 20**; seats opening each hand **10 falling to 3 → ten on every one of eighteen hands**; hands opened **8-to-20 unevenly → 18 on all ten seats**. And the number this row is about: sealed-to-*your-turn* at ten seats, hand #3 **8.0–15.3 s → 1.5–3.7 s**, hand #4 **6.3–19.7 s → 1.0–2.9 s**. **So the flat action budget is not currently binding, and no rules change is needed for it.** Four seconds against twenty-five is not a tight margin, and the proposal to derive `action_timeout_ms` from the seat count — which is a `table_params_hash` quantity and therefore the owner's — is **withdrawn as unnecessary rather than deferred**. The observation that the hand budget is seat-scaled and the action budget is not remains true, and remains worth revisiting if a slower link ever makes it bind. **What stays true from this row:** the attrition mechanism is real and was exactly as described — a seat certified out for a silence it had no chance to break, readmitted, and certified out again — and the table did shrink from ten seats to three until the lag fitted the budget. It was driven by a defect rather than by a mis-sized constant. | CLOSED: `src/protocol/constants.rs` (`action_timeout_ms` is not seat-scaled where `hand_deadline_min_ms` is), `src/table/hand.rs` (`on_timeout_cert`) — **answered, and the proposal withdrawn rather than deferred**: the 19.7 s was `S1-AU`'s certificate churn and not delivery scaling with the seat count, and across that fix the same measurement reads 1.0-2.9 s. The observation that the hand budget is seat-scaled and the action budget is not stays on the record and is worth revisiting if a slower link ever makes it bind. *(This row was tokened `OPEN` on 2026-09-06 off its headline and corrected the same day off its body — which is the reading the token pass was for.)* |
| S1-AU | **A seat the table had certified out went on voting — for itself — and sealed that vote into a certificate every other seat then refused. 443 of 443 refusals in one run come from it, and it is the cause of `S1-AT`'s attrition.** `on_timeout_vote` refuses a vote from a seat outside `voters(subject)`: *“if !self.voters(body.subject_seat).contains(&seat)”*. The **emit** side had no such test. `vote_on_timeouts` guards that the seat is not the subject, that it has not voted already, and that the voter set clears the floor of two — and never that this client is itself a voter — then `take_vote` inserts `my_seat` unconditionally. `certify_if_unanimous` sees `held = \|voters\| + 1`, seals, and broadcasts a certificate carrying a voter nobody else has in `dealt_in`, which every receiver refuses at `c.voters.is_subset(&nominal)`. **The fingerprint is a tally over its own denominator**: `4/3`, `7/6`, `8/7`, `9/8`, `5/4` — always exactly one too many, always this client's own. In `split215300-10` the 443 refusals are **394 from seat 2, 25 from seat 0, 24 from seat 9**, against roster narrowings of `certified [2]`, `[4]`, `[7]`, `[8]` and `[9, 0]`. Every refusal from a seat that had been certified out; none from anywhere else. **FIXED**, by the same predicate the receive path already enforces, applied where the vote is made. **And the reason nothing caught it is the reason to keep the test that now does.** At three seats, certifying one out leaves one voter and the two-voter floor refuses the vote *before* this question is asked; at five it leaves three, the floor passes, and the guard is reached. Every table-opening test in this repo used two or three seats. The new test opens **five**, and was verified by disabling the guard. | FIXED: `src/table/hand.rs` (`vote_on_timeouts`) |
| S1-AV | **A fragmented send that fails part-way rewinds that peer's whole message-id stream by up to 65 535, silently and permanently, past the first 65 536 messages. One truncated declaration.** `gcc_send_lossless_packet_fragments` opened with `const uint16_t start_id = gconn->send_message_id;` and `send_message_id` is a **`uint64_t`**. The truncation is harmless everywhere the value is used as an **index** — `gcc_get_array_index` is `% GCC_BUFFER_SIZE` and `65536 % 2048 == 0`, so the array walk is bit-for-bit identical — which is exactly why it survives a reading. But it is not only an index. **`clear_send_queue_id_range` ends with `gconn->send_message_id = start_id;`**, so on the unwind path the stream is set back to `send_message_id % 65536`. Past the first 65 536 messages to a peer that is a jump **backwards** of up to 65 535: every later message reuses an id the receiver has already consumed, the receiver discards them all as duplicates, and that peer is broken for the rest of the session with no error logged anywhere on either side. **Reachable exactly where this project has already been.** The unwind runs only when `add_to_send_array` fails part-way through a fragmented send — a full send ring — and `split203824-10` recorded **800** such failures in one ten-seat run before `S1-AR`'s fragment sizing. What has not happened yet is the other half: the highest id reached in a ten-minute run was about **25 000**, so the wrap has never been hit here. A longer table, or a busier one, arrives at 65 536. **FIXED**, `uint16_t` to `uint64_t`, as `patches/0008` with its own marker. Nothing else changes: no wire bytes, no timing, no index. **And it is worth naming why it hid.** Every one of its uses is correct. The defect is a single assignment fifty lines away in a different function, in the one path — an unwind after a failure — that a healthy run never takes. It was found by reading, not by measurement, and nothing in this repo's instrumentation would have caught it before it did the damage: a rewound stream produces silence, not an error. | FIXED: `vendor/c-toxcore/toxcore/group_connection.c` (`gcc_send_lossless_packet_fragments`, `clear_send_queue_id_range`), `patches/0008` |
| S1-AW | **Every message drained from the receive array was acknowledged with id 0, because the entry is wiped before its id is read — so the sender's slot was never cleared and the blind whole-window retransmission was quietly doing the acknowledgement's job.** `process_recv_array_entry` calls `clear_array_entry`, which does `*array_entry = (GC_Message_Array_Entry){nullptr}` and zeroes the whole struct including `message_id` — and **both** `gc_send_message_ack` calls read `array_entry->message_id` **after** it. `gcc_handle_ack` then looks up index 0, finds an empty slot or a different id, logs *“Wrap-around on message 0”* and returns false. The sender's entry for the message that actually arrived is never cleared, its `time_added` never moves, and `gcc_resend_packets` drops the peer once the head entry is `GC_CONFIRMED_PEER_TIMEOUT` = 58 s old. **And this is why removing the tail retransmission would have been fatal.** The only thing that rescued such an entry was a later blind resend producing a duplicate of an already-consumed message, which takes the `lossless_ret == 0` path and acks with the correct id. So the whole-window resend was not a repair pump, as an earlier reading of this session claimed, and not waste, as the reading before that claimed — it was the **send array's garbage collector**, standing in for a broken acknowledgement. Two independent adversarial readings concluded that `gcc_resend_packets` head-only would time every peer out on a healthy link; this is the reason, and the change was not made. **Fingerprint: 108 lines of “Wrap-around on message 0” in `split101212-10`, and zero in the run before it** — not because the defect was absent but because `patches/0009` had not landed, so the receive array drained far less often and this path ran far less often. 0009 did not cause it; it exposed it. **FIXED** as `patches/0010`: read the id into a local before the clear. Measured after: **108 → 0**. | FIXED: `vendor/c-toxcore/toxcore/group_connection.c` (`process_recv_array_entry`), `patches/0010` |

It is now 2 048, that bound exactly. Measured after: **drops 22 bursts → 0**. | FIXED: `src/tox/table.rs` (the inbox channel and its drop site), `src/net/run.rs` (it is now reported when it moves) |
| S1-AY | **The two test machines are not comparable, and every “the far seats are behind” observation in this register needs that caveat.** `172.16.0.20` reports **4 logical processors**; this machine reports **24**. `tools/table-run-split.ps1` puts **five full clients on each** — each one a toxcore thread, a libp2p swarm and ten-way mental-poker cryptography — so the far host runs 1.25 clients per core against 0.21 here. **It is measurable and it is not packet loss.** In `split101212-10` all 3 074 dropped hand events were on far nodes and the five local nodes dropped none; the far nodes' hand counts collapsed to 7, 7 and 10 while the local five held 16. Over the same four runs receive-ring failures fell **9 635 → 1 141 → 674 → 8**, three orders of magnitude, while the far five stayed pinned between 11 and 14 whatever the transport did. The local half now plays more than twice as many hands as the far half on identical software over the same link. **CONFIRMED BY THE OBVIOUS EXPERIMENT, and it settles the question.** `split112812-10`, the same binary and the same 900 seconds, **eight seats here and two there**: the two far seats opened **33 and 34** hands, level with the best local ones, against **11 to 14** when five were on that host. The far machine is not slow, it was **overloaded**. Everything else moved with it: peer timeouts **3 → 0**, settlement disagreements **553 → 64**, sealed-to-your-turn at ten seats **3.2–7.6 s → 0.7–1.6 s**, and 34 hands opened with all ten seats present throughout. So a large part of what this register has attributed to the protocol across four runs was the rig. **The distinction matters because the lossy two-machine rig is deliberate and must not be “fixed”** — it is the only place packet loss is reproducible, and that is an owner decision on record. CPU starvation is a different thing: it does not simulate the internet, it distorts the measurement, and it is the reason a run can look like a protocol regression when the protocol did not change. A split that respects the hosts — eight seats here and two there, or five and five with fewer seats — would separate the two. **Not changed unilaterally: the experiment design is the owner's.** | OPEN: `tools/table-run-split.ps1` — **open, and it is a question about the rig rather than the code** |
| S1-AZ | **Every peer disagrees with several others about the settlement of dozens of hands per run — and the disagreement is confined, in every single instance, to `state_hash`. Not one chip disagrees anywhere. It has been happening all along and this register had never looked.** `on_settlement` recomputes the whole `HandComplete` and compares it as a whole: *“a body that differs anywhere means two engines disagree about the hand, which is a divergence and not a preference”*. When it differs it reports the field and returns `Failed::DeckDisagrees`, so the settlement stage does not complete between those two peers. **Measured across four 900-second ten-seat runs: 269, 323, 654 and 553 disagreements, on all ten nodes in three of the four.** In `split110827-10` the per-node counts run from 33 to 83. And of those 553, the field is `checkpoint-8 state hash` **553 times out of 553**; the count of disagreements mentioning stacks, deltas, pots, refunds or busted seats is **zero**. **So the money is consistent and the membership is not.** `PROTOCOL.md` §6.1 puts `signed_this_hand` inside `state_hash` deliberately, *“so that agreement there is agreement about `P` itself”* — which makes a `state_hash`-only disagreement exactly a disagreement about **who took part**, and nothing else. Two peers computing the same pots, the same deltas and the same final stacks, and refusing each other's settlement because they hold different opinions about which seats were dealt in. **It is the same root as `S1-AQ`, seen from the settlement instead of the vote.** **CORRECTION, and it is this row's own headline number.** Filed at *“dozens of hands per run on every node”* on the strength of 553 in `split110827-10`. The very next run, `split112812-10`, same binary and same duration but **eight seats here and two on the far host instead of five and five**, recorded **64**. So roughly seven eighths of that churn was `S1-AY`'s CPU starvation — seats descheduled, missing their turn, certified out, readmitted — and not the protocol. The defect is real, 64 is not zero, and it is **an order of magnitude smaller than this row first said**. **Open, and it is `Q-10`'s.** Nothing here is an implementation choice: `signed_this_hand` is inside a hash every peer must derive identically, so making them agree means deciding what counts into a participation set, which is the question `K-1` recorded as unclosable by ratification and `S1-O` and `S1-AQ` both wait on. What this row adds is the price, which was not previously known: it is not a theoretical corner, it happens continuously, and the only reason it has not cost a chip is that `state_hash` is the one field the engines do not use to move money. | OPEN: `src/table/hand.rs` (`on_settlement`), `PROTOCOL.md` §6.1 — **open, and it is `Q-10`** |
| S1-BA | **A certified-out seat can never return, and not because the door is missing — because the door's key is the one thing being out makes impossible. Measured end to end, and it is the sharpest statement of `Q-10` this register has.** `S1-O` records that both readmission doors are built and that what is left is *“one sentence”*. The doors are built. **Door 2 cannot open for a certified-out seat, by construction:** §4.9's readmission set `A` is written by `checkpoint_event`'s `Took::Bystander` arm, whose own comment is *“a copy from a seat **outside** `P(k)` whose value **agrees**”*. The value is checkpoint-8's `state_hash`, and `PROTOCOL.md` §6.1 puts `signed_this_hand` inside it deliberately, *“so that agreement there is agreement about `P` itself”*. So to be readmitted a seat must agree about who took part in a hand it did not take part in — which is the exact fact that got it certified out. Its hash cannot agree, it never becomes a `Bystander`, and it is never readmitted. **Measured, `split112812-10`, the fair-rig run (eight seats here, two on the far host).** All **64** settlement disagreements in the run belong to exactly **two** nodes, **32 each** — and those two are the two that were certified out, `certified [8, 3]`, seen identically by every node. Each disagrees with the other **eight** seats and **not with each other**. They opened **25** hands against the others' **33-34**: a two-seat continuation of a ten-seat table, running in parallel, indefinitely. **Readmissions in the whole run: zero.** **And the seat knows.** `n3` logs *“cert: 8 votes held and none certified; subject Some(3)”* — it holds a unanimous set of votes against itself and correctly refuses to seal them, because `certify_if_unanimous` says *“Never about itself”*: the subject must **accept** a certificate naming it and must not **emit** one. It then applies the roster change from a peer at 722.4 s, so it agrees it is out. It simply has no way back. **This is not the monotone-roster objection and it is worth separating from it.** `R(k+1) ⊆ R(k)` would be a *policy* that keeps a seat out; this is a *fixed point*: the admission test and the reason for exclusion are the same quantity. Any repair has to break that identity — admit on something that does not commit to participation, or define agreement about `signed_this_hand` so that a bystander can hold it. Both are decisions about what counts into a participation set, which is `Q-10` exactly, and both are the owner's. **CORRECTION, 2026-09-05: the fixed point is gone, and the conclusion survives it.** `signed_this_hand` was field 28 of the public table state and it was deleted three hours after this row was filed (`041c39e`), because it was an observation of the listener rather than a fact about the hand; PROTOCOL.md 4.9 was rewritten to say so. The value a bystander must agree on therefore no longer commits to who took part, the door does open, and it was measured open: in `split125944-9` a certified-out seat's checkpoint agreed at every one of the six hands it followed, on every local node, and each node wrote it into `A`. What keeps that seat out is now the plain policy this row was at pains to distinguish itself from - the monotone roster - so the sharper statement of `Q-10` this row claimed to be has expired. Its measurement stands as the cost: `split112812-10`, 64 settlement disagreements, all of them the two certified-out seats, zero readmissions. | CLOSED: `src/net/run.rs` (`checkpoint_event`'s `Took::Bystander`), `src/table/hand.rs` (`certify_if_unanimous`), `PROTOCOL.md` 4.9, 6.1 - **superseded by `S1-BV`: the outcome holds, the reason given for it does not** |
| S1-BB | **The seat certified out for letting its clock run had acted instantly on every turn it was ever given. In `split112812-10`, hand #24, seat 8 (`n4`): `656.1 your turn — 400 to call` → `autoplay: Call` **in the same logged instant**; `657.8 your turn` → `autoplay: Check`, same instant; `659.1 your turn` → `autoplay: Check`, same instant. Three streets, three actions, **zero delay on any of them**. At **690.7 s** `n0` logged *“my clock has run out on seat 8”* and at 726.9 the table certified `[8, 3]` out. **And the two ends disagree about what is owed, not about how fast it came.** `n0`'s heartbeat at 690.2 reads `hand waiting for [8]`, so it believes seat 8 owes an action. Seat 8 was never prompted for a fourth one — from its own side it is the party waiting. Neither is idle and neither is slow; they hold different opinions about which street the hand is on. **What this is not.** It is not CPU starvation — this is the fair-rig run where the two far seats matched the best local ones. It is not the action budget being tight — sealed-to-your-turn in the same run was 0.7 to 2.1 s against 25 s. It is not a stalled peer: the ejected seat answered every prompt in the same millisecond it arrived. **What is not yet distinguished, and it matters which.** Either seat 8's third action was lost and never repaired — plausible as a **tail-of-burst** loss, where the last message a seat sends has no successor, so the receiver never sees a gap, never asks, and only the sender's blind retry can recover it — or the two engines diverged about the betting round itself. The first is a transport gap with a known shape; the second is a state divergence. This row does not claim which, and the run as logged cannot separate them. **Why it is worth more than the count suggests.** Two seats of ten in 900 s is a small rate, but `D-026` forbids a player ending a hand it is losing, and the mirror of that is that an honest player must not lose its seat for a silence it never committed. Combined with `S1-BA` — once out, never back — a single false positive is permanent. | OPEN: `src/table/hand.rs` (the action deadline and `waiting_for`), `src/net/run.rs` — **open, and the next measurement is to separate a lost tail-of-burst action from a divergent betting state** |
| S1-BC | **A third of the accusations that remove a player from a table are provably false: the seat spoke at the very stage it is accused of ignoring. The clock for “you did not speak” is being used for “I did not accept what you said”, and it completes the chain `S1-AZ → S1-BB → S1-BA`.** `S1-BB` left one binary question the logs could not answer, so the accusation was made to carry its evidence: the stage this client is at, against the stage it last accepted anything from the accused seat. `split130115-10` answers it, and the answer is **both**: **26 accusations name a seat last heard 7 to 9 stages earlier** — genuinely silent at this stage, a message that did not arrive — and **14 name a seat last heard at the accusing client's own current stage**. The clearest single line is `far-n0` at 611.5 s: *“my clock has run out on seat 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 — I am at stage 56, 2 last heard at stage 56, 3 last heard at stage 56, … 8 last heard at stage 56”*. **Seven seats accused of ignoring a stage all seven were heard at.** That is 35 % of every accusation in the run. **The mechanism, and it is four lines apart in one function.** `on_settlement` calls `note_signed(seat)` — which marks the seat heard and, since this session, records the stage — and **then**, nineteen lines later, compares the recomputed body and returns `Err(Failed::DeckDisagrees)` when it differs. `stage.hear(seat, …)` is never reached. So a seat whose settlement disagrees is **heard but not counted**: `waiting_for()` still names it, the action clock runs out on it, it is voted late, certified out — and by `S1-BA` it can never come back. **Which closes the chain.** `S1-AZ`'s `state_hash`-only settlement disagreements are not an inert bookkeeping curiosity; they are the input to this. A peer that computes the same pots, the same deltas and the same final stacks, and differs only in who it heard, is removed from the table for silence it never committed — permanently. **AND IT IS NOT ONE FUNCTION'S SLIP — the pattern is every handler.** All eleven `note_signed` call sites put it before the validation that can reject the event, and each has error returns between: `on_showdown` **8**, `on_shuffle_proof` **7**, `on_action` **6**, `on_board_reveal` **4**, `on_deal_private`, `on_shuffle_step` and `on_hand_complete` **3** each. Checked concretely outside the settlement: `on_deal_private` marks the seat heard, returns `BadToken` four lines later, and reaches `stage.hear` eighty lines further on — so a seat sending a malformed token is heard-but-not-counted exactly as a disagreeing settlement is. **Which rules out moving `note_signed`, and is the useful half of this.** Its doc says *“Note that a seat has signed something this hand”*, and that is **true and correct**: `signed_this_hand` means signed something, not contributed acceptably, and §6.1 hashes it. Moving the call would change the meaning of a hashed field to repair a report. The conflation is on the accusation side — `waiting_for()` and the action clock read *“has not contributed to this stage”* and the log says *“its clock has run out”* — and that is where a repair belongs. **Deliberately not fixed here.** Excluding a seat with `last_heard_at == stage_sequence` from timeout accusations would stop the false positives and is arguably just refusing to state a falsehood — but it decides when a player loses their seat, which is `D-026`'s subject from the other side, and it leaves the disagreement itself unresolved where removal currently ends it. The right terminus for *“we disagree”* is §6.3's freeze and checkpoint 8, not the action clock. That is the owner's call and it wants attacking before it ships. | OPEN: `src/table/hand.rs` (`on_settlement`, `note_signed`, `waiting_for`), `src/net/run.rs` (the accusation now carries its evidence) — **open, and it is `D-026` and `Q-10` meeting** |
| S1-BD | **FIXED by deleting a `return`, and the fix is smaller than the diagnosis.** The whole of it is that `on_hand_complete` returned `Err(DeckDisagrees)` **before** `stage.hear`, so a disagreeing settlement never entered its stage — and everything downstream follows from that one line: the stage never completed, `close_settlement_if_done` never ran, `checkpoint8` stayed `None`, no checkpoint-8 `STATE_HASH` was emitted, and the two differing values never met at the checkpoint built to compare them. The disagreement is now **noted and heard**: the report that names the differing field is kept, and the body goes into the stage. **Hearing it cannot move a chip, which is what makes this safe to do.** `close_settlement_if_done` applies `mine.final_stacks` — this client's OWN settlement — and publishes this client's own `state_hash`; it never adopts the other seat's numbers. So a differing copy only lets the stage close, and closing the stage is what publishes the checkpoint where §6.3 can act. **The pot is awarded instead of restored, which is the half `D-026` was losing by doing nothing.** **One sibling was deliberately left alone as the next step, and is taken — with a measurement between — as `S1-BP`.** `on_late_settlement` has the identical refusal-before-hear, but it also seeds `late.body` from **whichever peer speaks first**, and those stacks reach `next_hand()` and hence `GENESIS(k+1)` — a D-012 question in its own right. Changing both paths at once, on the riskiest item in the set, without a measurement between them, is the thing this register keeps recording the cost of. **ORIGINAL DIAGNOSIS.** §6.3's freeze is the one terminus the corpus provides for *“two peers disagree”*, it is fully implemented, and it has never once fired — because the refusal that detects the disagreement is also what stops it being reached. Meanwhile the hand is ended by abort and the pot is never awarded, which is `D-026` violated by doing nothing.** **Measured, `split130115-10`, 900 s, ten seats: freeze/reconcile lines 0. Settlement disagreements 124.** The mechanism is a chain of omissions, each individually reasonable: `on_hand_complete` refuses a disagreeing body and returns before `stage.hear`, so the settlement stage never completes; so `close_settlement_if_done` never runs; so `checkpoint8` stays `None`; so no checkpoint-8 `STATE_HASH` is emitted; so the two differing values never meet at the checkpoint that exists to compare them. `Took::Diverged` and the `frozen` path in `run.rs` are live code that this table cannot reach. **And the current behaviour is not a neutral baseline.** Hand #20 ended on every node between 607.0 and 614.0 s and it ended by **abort** — ten abort lines, no settlement anywhere. The pot was restored, not awarded. So *“do nothing”* already produces the outcome `D-026` forbids: a hand in progress cancelled and nobody paid, with a permanent two-camp fork on top. That disposes of the fourth candidate on the evidence rather than on preference. **Four repairs were designed and each attacked from three angles; none survived.** The best two carried one fatal or serious attack out of three, the rest carried three of three. The sharpest single objection is against the obvious one: moving `note_signed` below the disagreement check *“deletes the `last_heard_at` evidence that makes the falsehood visible, converting a provably false accusation into an indistinguishable one while changing no outcome”* — the instrument built to measure `S1-BC` would be the first casualty of the fix for it. **Nothing was shipped.** **What the readings agree on, and it is a direction rather than a patch.** The accusation is not an error of the clock; it is the clock being asked a question the settlement handler refused to answer. Making the freeze reachable is what the corpus itself points at — and it was attacked too, because a frozen table is also a `D-026` problem. So the choice is between three bad terminuses for one disagreement: remove a seat that spoke, freeze the table, or stall the hand. **All three are the owner's, and this row's contribution is that the fourth option — leave it alone — is now measured and is the worst of them.** | OPEN: `src/table/hand.rs` (`on_hand_complete`, `close_settlement_if_done`), `src/net/run.rs` (`Took::Diverged`, the `frozen` path), `PROTOCOL.md` §6.3, §6.4 — **open, and it is `D-026` and `Q-10` meeting** |
| S1-BE | **Confirmed on the hard split too, and it turned up the better outcome: with the false certification gone, a starved peer is caught by the detection that was designed for it and withdraws instead of running a table of its own.** `split153620-10`, five seats here and five on the four-core host — the configuration `S1-AY` shows is CPU-bound, chosen deliberately as the harder case. **Settlement disagreements 553 → 0** against the last 5+5 run before the change, and **false timeout accusations 0**. The removal of §6.1 field 28 is a property of the compared value, not of load, and it holds under load. **And the shape of the failure changed for the better.** Two far seats fell behind, and each logged *“this client is out: the table is at hand 4 and this client reached only 2, so it has been dealing a hand nobody else has… but hand 2's checkpoint has gone out, so the others are not held up by this”* — §3.2's solitary-stage rule, firing correctly and stopping them. **Withdrawals: 2, where every earlier run recorded 0.** **Why it fired only now, and it is the point.** Before, stragglers were *certified out in pairs* — `certified [8, 3]` — so neither was solitary, each had the other, and they ran a two-seat continuation of a ten-seat table for 25 hands (`S1-BA`). Remove the false certification and a starved peer is simply alone, which is the case the solitary-stage rule exists for. **Taking out the wrong detector let the right one work.** **What this run does not show, said plainly.** Only 14 hands, and receive-ring failures **8 → 20 433** against the previous 5+5 run — 19 299 of them on the far machine, against 46–426 on each local node, with both two-hand seats far. The far host was verified clean afterwards: no leftover processes, 3 % load. **That swing is inside the observed variance of this configuration** — 5+5 has produced 4, 8, 14, 16, 19, 31 and 32 hands from identical binaries today — and **it is not attributed here to anything specific, because it cannot be**. It is not the field removal: that changes what is hashed and cannot reach toxcore's receive ring. | CLOSED: — **confirmation, and `S1-AY` again** |
| S1-BF | **WITHDRAWN BEFORE IT WAS ACTED ON: the “stable 0.8-second-per-peer stagger” was this harness's own clock. Each node's timestamp column ran from its own start, and the harness starts them up to seven seconds apart in exactly the order the stagger claimed.** The claim was that peers open each hand in a fixed order, `n7` first in **33 hands of 33**, with average lateness 0.0 / 0.8 / 1.4 / 2.0 / 2.6 / 3.2 / 4.1 / 4.4 / 5.6 / 7.1 s — a clean monotonic gradient, stable across every hand, accounting for most of the eight-second cryptographic phase. It looked like a real per-peer cost and it was about to be treated as one. **It is a measurement artefact and the arithmetic is exact.** Every seat runs in its own `Start-Job` with `$start = Get-Date` captured *inside* the job, so each log counts from that node's own zero — and the loop sleeps **3 s** after the founder and **400 ms** after each later seat. Reconstructing the origins from message causality alone gives `n0` 0.00, `far-n0` 1.54, `far-n1` 3.06, `n1` 3.22, `n2` 3.87, `n3` 4.48, `n4` 5.09, `n5` 5.69, `n6` 6.36, `n7` 6.98 — and `n7 − X` reproduces the reported lateness **to within 0.2 s, node for node**. **With the origins removed there is no stagger at all:** all ten peers open each hand within **0.2 s**, which is one delivery, and the order reshuffles nearly every hand. **And the check that should have caught it needed no arithmetic.** Every node ran `--for 900` and played **exactly 33 hands**, mean gap 25.8 to 26.0 s on all ten. A peer genuinely seven seconds behind cannot finish level. `n7`'s hand #33 opens at its 892.6 and `n0`'s at its 899.5 — the same seven-second “lead” at the *end* of the run as at the start, which is the signature of an offset and not of a rate. **FIXED where it belongs, in the instrument.** Both ends now stamp UTC beside the elapsed figure; the two machine clocks were measured 57 ms apart, so the columns are directly comparable. **Two related readings were confirmed and are worth keeping so they are not re-proposed.** The per-peer send walk in `gc_send_custom_packet` costs about **6 microseconds** an iteration — one `add_to_send_array`, one AEAD seal of ≤ 500 B, one non-blocking `sendto` — so 0.8 s per rank would need it to be **185 000×** slower than it measures, and would make the run's own 33 hands arithmetically impossible. And `flush` adds at most one 50 ms driver tick: where *“the table's transport is behind: N message(s) queued”* is printed with **0 fragments refused**, N is the entry snapshot of a burst that the same call drained, not a backlog. Lines with a non-zero refusal count — `far-n0`'s 404 queued against 814 refused — are genuine and are not withdrawn. **This is the third measurement-validity failure of the session**, after a binary built without the instrumentation it was read for and a packet type read backwards. All three had the same shape: a number that was never measured, read as a fact. | CLOSED: `tools/table-run-split.ps1` (both timestamp sites) |
| S1-BG | **A `JOIN_ACCEPT` that loses its race with the founder's own broadcast rewinds the joiner's roster to the table as it stood when it was seated — permanently, because that write is the one membership mutation that moves no version.** `on_join_answer` ended `self.roster = roster;` unconditionally. The snapshot is `roster_so_far`, built by the founder at the instant of acceptance, so it holds exactly `seat + 1` entries. The other three sites that assign `self.roster` all move the serial — `on_join_request` and `release_seat_before_the_first_hand` do `serial += 1`, `on_player_list` is gated by `admit_list`'s `NotNewer` — while this one left `serial`, `ratified` and `session` untouched, and `JoinAcceptBody` carries no `list_serial` at all, so the snapshot cannot even be *ordered* against what is held. **It is not a race lost by microseconds — the reply is simply slow.** The peer that died asked to join at t=10.6 s and its `JOIN_ACCEPT` arrived at t=20.6 s; in those ten seconds the founder seated two more players and the joiner adopted lists all the way to the final serial 9. So the condition is narrow and stateable: **the accept arrives after the last `PLAYER_LIST` this peer will ever adopt, and the peer is not the last seat.** Exactly three peers met the first half and only the two that were not the last seat were harmed. **And there is no way back — including down the path built for exactly this.** `Formation::say_again` *is* a full-state anti-entropy push: it re-signs and re-emits the complete `PlayerList`, and `run.rs` calls it on every gossipsub `Subscribed` so that a seat which missed one can be repaired. It re-emits at the **current** serial, which is the one the clobbered peer already holds, so it dies at `list_serial <= held` like every other rebroadcast. The repair machinery exists, is reached, and is disarmed by the same equality. The peer sits on a roster nobody else holds until it is killed. **Measured at ten seats over 900 s, and the prediction held with no exceptions: every clobbered peer's roster fell to exactly `my_seat + 1`.** Seat 7 fell 10 -> 8, printed `ratified 5/8, 10 held` twenty-nine times, never sealed a table, played 0 hands and exited `NO TABLE` after 880 s. Seat 8 fell 10 -> 9 **100 ms after it had already sealed** — and since only `adopt` clears `session`, it kept a session whose roster hash nobody else computes, which is why it opened all 14 of its hands about 11.8 s after everybody else and finished none of them. Seat 9 was untouched because by then the snapshot was the whole table, and the early joiners recovered because a higher serial still followed. **And it has a clean control.** The run was repeated on the *same binary*, unchanged, and that time no accept landed late: all ten seats sealed, every roster stayed at ten — and the table played **41 hands, 40 finished**, against 15 in the run with two clobbered peers. Same code, same two machines, same 900 s; the only difference is whether this fired. **Two sick peers cost the table two thirds of its game.** **It is rare and it is not random:** across 40 node-runs it fired twice, both times on the peer it killed, and never on a peer that survived. **Found by measuring, not by reading.** It surfaced only once both machines stamped a comparable clock (`S1-BF`): nine of the ten peers open a hand within 0.02-0.42 s of each other — the two on the far machine among them, at 0.39 and 0.40 — and the tenth at 11.83 s. That single outlier was the whole of the median 11.42 s spread, and it is what pointed here. **FIXED, without a wire change:** take the seat from the accept always, and its roster only when what is held does not already seat us where the founder says. That covers both directions — a stale snapshot cannot rewind a list, and a list that does not yet contain us cannot leave us seated but absent from our own roster. `a_late_accept_does_not_rewind_a_roster` reproduces it deterministically and fails without the guard, with the roster falling 3 -> 2. **The structural fix is still open and is a wire change:** `JoinAcceptBody` has four fields where `PlayerListBody` and `TableReadyBody` both carry `list_serial`. Until it does, the two rosters can only be ranked by a proxy, never compared. | FIXED: `src/net/formation.rs` (`on_join_answer`), `src/net/joinwire.rs` (`JoinAcceptBody`, open) |
| S1-BH | **A seat is certified out *because* it cannot hear the table's Tox group, and the certificate that removes it is a hand message, so it rides that same group. The one party who most needs to know is the one party structurally guaranteed not to be told.** It is not a bug in `publish_hand`: that function has no `else` on purpose — a hand never touches libp2p, by instruction and by capacity — and the certificate is a hand message like any other. The gap is that **nothing else tells it either**, on any channel, ever. **Measured, ten seats, 900 s, 41 hands.** Three seats were certified out. Seat 2 never entered the group at all (`group 0 seen/0 confirmed/9 wanted` with `tox friends up 8`, unchanged for 27 of 29 samples), was certified out, and logged **not one mention of it** — the string does not appear in its file. It exited printing `TABLE FORMED session=d4c1af72 seats=10`. **For fifteen minutes it told its user it was sitting at a full ten-handed table it had already been removed from.** **A first draft of this row said recovery was “proved twice in this run”, and that claim is withdrawn: it rested on hand counts, and a hand count does not measure participation.** A certified-out peer keeps following the table and keeps logging `hand #k opens` — with a seat list that does not contain it. Seat 3 opened hand 25 at 721.6 s with `seats [0, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9]`, having been struck at 554.5 s. So “played 25 hands” was really “watched up to hand 25”. The same mistake made the two removals look like they happened *while the seats were still arriving*; they were in fact certified out after nine and twelve minutes of play, having entered the group at 30.3 s and 46.9 s. **What the readmission log actually shows, once the right string is grepped.** §4.9 readmission announces itself with the words *“are heard again at this hand”* — which is the stable part of a line whose tail has since been corrected (`S1-BV`) and is what a grep should key on; the word `readmit` appears only in test assertions, which is why a first search found nothing and nearly became a finding of its own. Against that string: one run readmitted seat 3 three times, another readmitted seat 4 once, and **a third certified two seats out and never heard either again**. So readmission is real, is reached, and is not reliable — and a seat that is readmitted repeatedly is *flapping*, losing and regaining its place, which is its own cost and is not the same as recovering. That is measured, not explained. **The removal itself is right and must stay.** Nine players cannot wait on one, and `S1-AA`'s guard behaved exactly as designed: seat 2 refused to deal into a void rather than opening hands that finish never, and said so — *“the table's group is not filling … nothing can be dealt until it fills.”* The client already knows. It simply does not act on what it knows. **FIXED, and it needed no message at all — which is the point.** Every peer that CAN hear the group receives its own certificate and applies it; measured, seat 7 was struck at 501.4 s and had opened the next hand *without itself in the seat list* by 503.6 s. **The only peer left uninformed is the one that hears nothing, and that peer can see its own silence.** So the fix is entirely local: no wire change, no new message, no protocol addition, and the owner's rule that a hand never touches libp2p is not even approached. **Three things had to change, and each of them is why the client knew and never said.** The sentence that states this exactly — *“not one of {want} other seats is in the table's group … this client is isolated and the other seats will certify it out”* — is a `NodeEvent::Warning`, and `is_advisory` marks those **droppable**; it lives inside `hand_one_may_open`, whose three call sites are all under `if !ever_dealt`, so it **stops being said at the moment a mid-table silence would matter**; and it never reached `AppState`, which is what the exit banner prints. Housekeeping now emits the same two numbers as a non-advisory `NodeEvent::Carrier { seen, want }` — that arm runs whatever else is happening — the seat record latches `silent_since` and **clears it the instant anybody is heard**, and `Seat::playing` is the one place the judgement is made. **`DEAF_MS` = 120_000, derived:** past `CARRIER_GIVES_UP_MS` = 58_000, past `CARRIER_LADDER_LAST_MS` = 33_000, and past a housekeeping interval of staleness on top — because the cost of being wrong here is telling a player who is fine that they are not. **`TABLE FORMED` is unchanged for a seat that is playing**, since it is the line every scripted run greps; a seat that formed a table and then heard nothing for `DEAF_MS` prints `NOT PLAYING session=… seats=… heard=…` instead. `table-run.ps1` now tells that outcome apart from *the seats never all arrived*, which were one sentence before. `a_client_that_hears_nobody_does_not_claim_to_be_playing` covers the latch, the transient that must not latch, and the recovery, and fails without the verdict. **Telling the removed peer is the other half and is the owner's.** It cannot be told over the group by definition; it *can* be told over libp2p, which it demonstrably still had — 8 subscribed and 8 connected lobby peers throughout — and D-019 already puts roster on libp2p, so a removal notice there would cross no line the instruction draws. That is a protocol addition and is not made here. | FIXED: `src/net/run.rs` (`publish_hand`, unchanged and correct), the client's own seated state (open) |
| S1-BI | **Leaving a table carried twelve fields into the next one, and because the next table restarts the hand-id sequence at 1, everything keyed by hand id meets the previous table's record under the same key.** Found by auditing the *class* of `S1-BG` rather than the bug: state protected by a version, written by a path that does not move it. Thirteen candidates were swept and **nine were refuted as cosmetic**; these are what survived an adversarial pass. **The reachable one needs no prior fault at all.** `ahead` holds a raw hand id per seat and is filled by ordinary play — the `ADRIFT_MARGIN` note in this same file records 36 of 134 runs with a seat exactly one hand behind from `Ended::pause` skew, and says a table of four or more always has at least two. Within one table those age out as ids rise. At the next table they are all in the future again, so the first ordinary skew event at hand 1 latches `adrift` on a client that never drifted — and **`adrift` has no clearing site anywhere in the tree**. `frozen` is worse: its only release needs `RoundTook::Resolved` for the *old* hand id, whose evidence is the old table's signed bytes carrying the old `table_id`, which no seat at the new table can verify — and a frozen client emits nothing, so the seats waiting on its `STATE_HASH` wait for ever. **It stalls the new table, not only this peer.** `boundaries` is admitted on the hand id alone (`Boundaries::holds` is `open.contains_key(&hand_id)`); the table is checked only afterwards, and although a `Boundary` already carries a `table_id`, it is read when sealing outgoing events and never to refuse an incoming one. **The shape of the old fix is why there was a new bug.** The teardown was four copies of one list of assignments, and the comment inside it records the previous instance: `ever_dealt` set true and never set back, so the next table refused every stranger, never opened hand 1, never re-said a ratification, never released a silent seat and was never re-advertised — *“five mechanisms, all by not running”*. That was fixed by adding one line to each of the four copies, which is precisely the fix that leaves the next field to be found the same way. **FIXED as one thing:** a `leave_the_table!()` macro holds the whole list once, all four sites call it, and the twelve missing fields are in it. Adding a per-table field is now one edit. 707 tests pass with no warnings. **And the claim that it forgot nothing was then checked rather than asserted — it had.** Enumerating all 75 locals declared ahead of `run()`'s loop and subtracting the macro's own list left three that are per table: `frozen_said`, whose own doc says *“cleared when the freeze is”* while the macro clears the freeze beside it — so the next table's first freeze would have been **silent**, the one state that stops a client dealing with nothing said about why — and `no_round_said` and `disputes_seen`, which both hang off that freeze and its reconciliation round. Twenty-nine fields now, and the enumeration is the check to repeat. `table`, `table_topic`, `tox_sink` and `table_announces` stay at the call sites, where a teardown that must run in order already handles them; `resend_at` and `resend_ticks` reset themselves on the first stage of any new hand; `have_reservation` has a lifecycle of its own and was left alone deliberately. **Two limits worth stating.** The `boundaries` half is a GUI-client defect only — the sole teardown that can leave a populated store is `LeaveTable`, issued from the Leave button; a headless soak never leaves, so no run in this register would ever have hit it. And the collision window there is one hand wide, because the next table's first boundary open destroys the stale record. The `ahead`/`adrift` half has neither limit. | FIXED: `src/net/run.rs` (`leave_the_table!`, four call sites) |
