| S1-BJ | **One advertisement, signed by anybody and legal under §7.2, emptied every table off the user's screen — and repeating it once every 30 seconds kept the screen empty for as long as the sender cared to.** The interface keeps its own copy of the lobby, deliberately, so that it does not drift from the node's silently. It has no wall clock of its own, so it took one from the advertisement being offered: `let now = ad.timestamp_unix_ms.max(self.newest_seen)` — and the very next line is `self.lobby.expire(now)`. **The arithmetic is the whole bug: `MAX_CLOCK_SKEW_MS` = 120_000 against `AD_TTL_MS` = 90_000 — two minutes of admissible skew against ninety seconds of lifetime.** Rule 5 admits a timestamp up to 120 s ahead, so an advert signed that far in the future carries the expiry clock with it and drops every record not received within 90 s of that — which is every honest table. The attacker's own table is then inserted, alone. `MAX_ADS_PER_PEER_PER_MIN` is 20, so the cost of holding the list empty is one message every 30 s, a sixth of the allowance. The node's own store is untouched, because it ages on the real clock; the two copies simply disagree, and the user sees the wrong one. **Reproduced before it was believed.** The audit's own refuter wrote a throwaway probe rather than reason about it: three honest tables seen, then one advert at `now + 120 s`, and the list printed the attacker's table and nothing else. That is also what makes it a weaker finding than `S1-BG` — honest founders re-broadcast and their rows come straight back, so a single advert is transient. It is sustained repetition that makes it an outage. **FIXED where the wrong clock was read, not where the constants are.** This layer *is* handed a real clock, in `Swept { now_ms }`, and it was already storing it as `last_sweep_ms` for the presence list. The expiry clock now uses that, and falls back to the advert's claim only before the first sweep has arrived, when there is genuinely nothing else. Monotone either way, which is all `offer` and `expire` require. `a_future_advert_does_not_sweep_the_screen` fails without it, with one table on screen where there should be three. **The constant relationship is left alone and is worth an owner's eye.** `MAX_CLOCK_SKEW_MS > AD_TTL_MS` is what made this reachable at all, and `constants.rs` asserts `AD_REBROADCAST_MS * 3 <= AD_TTL_MS` but nothing relating these two. Making the skew allowance smaller than the lifetime would close the shape rather than this one instance of it — but it is a protocol constant with its own reasoning, and changing it is not an implementation's call. | FIXED: `src/app/mod.rs` (`NodeEvent::TableSeen`), `src/protocol/constants.rs` (the inequality, open) |
| S1-BK | **A stage must finish in 30 s, and this transport's own worst-case repair of one lost message is 32 s. The protocol kills a seat two seconds before its carrier has finished trying to save it — and the two numbers were never set against each other.** `crypto_step_timeout_ms` is hard-coded at `30_000` (`run.rs`), and `past_stage_deadline` is exactly `now_ms - stage_at_ms >= next_deadline_for(owed)`. Toxcore retransmits an unacked send-array entry only when `delta` reaches a power of two on whole seconds — `delta > 1 && is_power_of_2(delta)` — so the attempts fall at **+2, +4, +8, +16 and +32 s** after the message was queued. A message that needs the last of those arrives **after** the stage that was waiting for it has already expired, and its sender is voted out for it. **Nothing is lost, which is why this hid.** `Send array stuck; timing out peer` fires when the head of the send array goes unacked for `GC_CONFIRMED_PEER_TIMEOUT` = 58 s; it appears **zero times on every node of both runs**, so every message was acknowledged in the end. The seats were not silent. They were late, by a schedule their own carrier chose. **A first draft of this row blamed the network, and one experiment disproved it. That claim is withdrawn.** `Failed to send packet` was logged 645 times by one remote seat, 23 by the other and 0 by all eight local ones, and that ranking matched participation exactly — 11 hands of 36, 32 of 36, 36 of 36 — which made *the cross-subnet link* the obvious culprit. It is not. **Re-run with one seat on the remote machine instead of two, over the same link, that seat logged ZERO send failures and was dealt into 29 hands of 29** — the best of the table. In the same run the only node that failed sends at all was a **local** one, 109 times, and it too played every hand; the two seats that lost theirs were both local, with zero failures each. So send failures neither require the network nor predict removal. **What they track is contention on the host.** The remote box is 4 cores and 7.9 GB against this machine's 24, and it was running two full clients, each with its own DHT; alone, it is flawless. At 9 seats here and 1 there the pressure moved to this machine and the failures moved with it. **That is the test rig, not the protocol** — ten real players have ten machines — and it means no run in this register has yet tested 30 s against 32 s on a genuinely lossy link. **The accusation's own words were misleading and cost an afternoon.** *“I am at stage 34, 7 last heard at stage 27”* reads as *we have not heard from this seat in seven stages*, and it sent the investigation after lag. `last_heard_at` is written in exactly one place, `note_signed`, and **enters no deadline predicate anywhere** — it is a diagnostic string appended to a vote the stage clock had already decided. Measured on a clock comparable across both machines, the accused seats were **not lagging at all**: their lateness in opening each hand was 0.00-0.23 s, the same as the locals, and one of them opened its final hand **first of all ten**. **FIXED, and the 30 s stayed — by making the repair fit the budget instead of widening the budget to fit the repair.** The owner's instruction was to keep the decision at 30 s and change whatever else had to change, toxcore included. Three parts, none of which touches a packet format, a packet type, a handshake or the retransmit ladder, so a stock client on the public Tox network cannot tell the difference: **(1) The stage tells the carrier what it is waiting for (`patches/0011`).** The blind ladder is not the carrier's only repair — `GR_ACK_REQ` is answered by an immediate retransmission with **no backoff at all**, one round trip, measured at 36 to 326 ms on this link. It is stock upstream toxcore (`group_chats.h:116`) and `patches/0002` already uses the identical call. It was simply never reachable here: a receiver asks only when it can SEE a hole, which needs a later message to have arrived, and a stage waiting on one seat has nothing later to reveal it. The application is the only party that knows, so the stall tick now asks each seat in `waiting_for()` to re-send. **Worst case falls from T+33 s to one round trip plus at most a second of toxcore's own throttle** — inside 30 s with twenty times the room to spare. **(2) A seat the carrier is mid-delivery with is late, not silent.** `gcc_recv_pending` counts that peer's messages sitting in the receive array: they arrived out of order, so the seat *is* sending and something earlier has not landed. The vote skips such a seat. Measured on the run that produced this row, the signal is exactly where it is needed — 545 and 169 on the two seats that were falsely accused, **0 on every seat that was not**. It reads local memory and sends nothing. **And it is bounded, because `D-026` is what the bound is for.** A seat could otherwise buy silence for ever by sending later messages while withholding the one a stage needs, so past `long_past_stage` — twice the budget, just beyond where the carrier gives a confirmed peer up at 58 s — the vote goes ahead whatever the carrier says. **(3) §7.2's floor is the carrier, not one second.** `admit` took anything from `1_000`, thirty-three times shorter than the blind ladder; it is now `CRYPTO_STEP_MIN_MS` = 30_000 to `CARRIER_GIVES_UP_MS` = 58_000, with `CARRIER_LADDER_LAST_MS` = 33_000 mirrored from the C beside two static assertions. **That is the place this row said did not exist: where the two numbers meet.** The ladder's own expression is now a checked patch marker in `tools/build-tox.ps1`, so editing it in C fails the build that depends on it. `a_seat_the_carrier_is_still_delivering_is_not_accused_yet` covers both the reprieve and its end, and fails without the guard. **MEASURED, and the path it reaches was all but dead before it.** Nine seats over two machines, 900 s, with the levers in: toxcore logs *“Re-sent requested packet”* each time it answers a `GR_ACK_REQ` with an immediate retransmission, and it did so **518 times on the remote seat and 146 to 203 times on every local one — 1 922 repairs in one run**. The same harness on the same machines *without* the nudge logged **0, 0 and 12**. So the carrier's one-round-trip repair was not merely under-used; it fired a dozen times by accident in fifteen minutes, and every other loss waited for the blind ladder. **Certificates issued: zero, on all nine nodes.** Nobody was voted out at all. And the table did not pay for it — **51 hands against the 44 of the baseline before any of this**, with `opened == finished` on every seat, so no hand was opened and abandoned. The remote seat played **51 of 51**, tied for the best at the table. The stage budget is still 30 000. **The first version of part 1 was measured and rejected, and the lesson is where the cost hid.** `tox_group_peer_request_missing` takes a public key, so the peer NUMBER is not wanted — but the first draft found one with `peer_for`, which walks `0..PEER_SCAN` = **64** calling `tox_group_peer_get_public_key`, and every one of those takes the Tox lock. At nine waiting seats on a two-second tick that is up to **576 lock-taking FFI calls per tick, on the thread whose only job is to pump the group's messages**. Measured: the table fell from the baseline's **44 hands in 900 s to 18**, and the one seat that was alone on its machine — and therefore not starved — ran eight hands ahead of the other eight, opening hands 19 to 26 that nobody else reached and finishing none of them. The driver already keeps `known_as`, so the same question is answered by a reverse lookup in a small map with no FFI and no lock at all. **A repair that costs the carrier more than the loss it repairs is not a repair**, and only a run says which it is. **Two instruments were repaired on the way, both silent failures of the kind this register keeps finding.** `patches/0006`'s marker named text the source does not contain — it omits the `(%s)` the log line carries — so the guard proving that patch is applied had **never matched**, on a patch that is applied. And `build.rs` tracked the vendored C by naming its two DIRECTORIES, with a comment asserting that cargo walks a directory; it does not, and editing a file inside one leaves the directory's mtime alone. Measured: after adding two entry points to `tox.c`, `cargo build` said `Finished` and `libtoxcore.a` was still the copy built four days earlier, with neither symbol in it. Every file is now named individually. The comment that was wrong had itself been written to warn about the previous instance of the same failure. **So what is left, stated at its true strength.** The arithmetic is verified and is a real latent mismatch: a stage budget of 30 s cannot survive a carrier whose last repair attempt lands at 32 s, and nothing in the tree relates the two numbers. The trigger being the stage clock rather than `last_heard_at`, the accused seats not lagging, and no message ever being lost are all verified by reading. **What is NOT established is that this mismatch caused any certification yet observed** — no single message was followed from a failed send through a +32 s retry into a specific vote, and the correlation that suggested it broke in the very next run. It is a fault waiting for a link bad enough to expose it, and the rig has not yet produced one; the thing it has produced is starvation, which is a different cause with the same symptom. | FIXED: `src/net/run.rs` (`crypto_step_timeout_ms`), `src/table/hand.rs` (`past_stage_deadline`), `vendor/c-toxcore/toxcore/group_connection.c` (`gcc_resend_packets`) |
| S1-BL | **Which seat gets certified out of a ten-handed table in this rig is decided by how many clients its machine is running, not by where it sits on the network — and that has been read the other way round more than once.** The split harness puts eight seats here and two on a second machine, and for three runs the seats that lost their place were the two remote ones: dealt into 16, 12 and 11 hands where the locals played 40, 42 and 36. `Failed to send packet` ranked the same way — 645, 23, and zero on all eight locals — and the cross-subnet link was the obvious answer. **One experiment settles it. Same link, same binary, one seat on the remote machine instead of two: it logged zero send failures and was dealt into 29 hands of 29, the best at the table.** The only node that failed a send that run was a **local** one, 109 times, and it also played every hand. The two seats that lost theirs were both local, with no send failures at all. So the remote position neither causes the failures nor predicts the removals, and the failures do not predict the removals either. **The machine does.** The far end is **4 logical cores and 7.9 GB** against this machine's 24; two full clients there, each running its own toxcore DHT and its own async runtime, is roughly two cores apiece, and that is where the failures were. Give it one and it is flawless. Move the pressure — nine seats here, one there — and the failures move here with it. **And the baseline closes it.** Nine seats with neither machine oversubscribed — eight here on 24 cores, one on the far box — ran 900 s, played **44 hands**, and **certified nobody out at all**: zero certificates across all nine logs, and a single accusation in the whole run which never reached unanimity. **Eight of the nine seats were dealt into all 44 hands, the remote one among them.** The ninth missed seven, because it entered the Tox group at 145 s and those hands were already over — arrival, not removal. So every certification this register has recorded from a ten-seat split run was starvation, and a seat across the subnet is indistinguishable from a seat on this machine once its host has room to run. **This is the rig, and the rig is not to be fixed** (D-020: the lossy two-machine bed is a good simulator and must stay). But it must be *read* correctly, and it changes how earlier rows should be read too: any run where one host carried more clients than it has cores is evidence about starvation first, and about the protocol only after that has been excluded. **The cheap control is to move a seat between the machines and see whether the symptom follows the seat or stays with the box.** It cost one 900-second run to overturn a diagnosis that three runs had agreed on. **Two smaller things came out of the same run.** The harness's `opened` column counted every hand a seat *watched*, including hands whose own seat list excluded it after it had been struck — a removed peer keeps following the table — so a seat out for three minutes still looked like a seat that was playing; it now counts hands the seat was dealt into, and `finished` counts the same ones, because two columns answering different questions produced rows like *opened 8, finished 22*. | FIXED: `tools/table-run-split.ps1` (`Read-Node`), and the reading of every ten-seat run in this register |
| S1-BM | **`Q-10` has an answer that neither horn of it offers, and the reason not to build it today is that the population it serves is now empty.** The question is whether `R(k+1)` comes from `R(k)` filtered — monotone, what ships, and what makes `D-013`'s *“one silent seat costs exactly one hand”* untrue as written — or from `P(k)` including §4.9's readmitted bystanders, which puts a **per-receiver** term into a genesis and is what `D-012` forbids outright. **The third answer is a return certificate, symmetric with the timeout certificate the tree already has.** `R(k+1) = R(k)` minus the subjects of complete `TIMEOUT_CERT`s **plus the subjects of complete `RETURN_CERT`s`** — both directions certificate-gated, so `P(k)` and the per-receiver set `A` never reach a required emitter set at all. A certificate is unanimous by construction, so its subject list is collective and hashing it into `GENESIS(k+1)` is exactly as safe as hashing `R(k)`. The evidence a return certificate votes on already exists and already runs: a seat outside `P(k)` that publishes a checkpoint-8 `STATE_HASH` **agreeing with the receiver's own** is §4.9's `A`, and since this session's checkpoint work that path runs end to end — it is per-receiver only because nothing ratifies it, and a unanimous vote is what ratifying it means. **Cost, stated honestly:** two new event types on the wire with their bodies, caps and anti-replay rows, the vote-and-seal machinery duplicated for the second direction, and corpus edits in `PROTOCOL.md` §4.9 and §8.3 and in `STATE_MACHINE.md`. That is the largest of the four decisions by a wide margin, and it is a protocol addition rather than a repair. **And it is a door for nobody at the moment.** A seat reaches the state readmission exists for only by being certified out, and after `S1-BK` the measured count of certificates is **zero** — across three consecutive 900-second runs at nine seats, 44, 51 and the current run's hands, with one accusation in total that never reached unanimity. Building two wire events for a population of none is the kind of work that is right in principle and speculative in fact. **The day this row asked for came on 2026-09-05** (`S1-BV`: a seat with a working link, agreeing with the table at six consecutive checkpoints, locked out of a tournament it had chips in), so the design was taken to four independent designers, three judges and two adversarial passes. **The answer is this row's own, confirmed in kind and corrected in five places; the full note is `docs/research/READMISSION.md`.** What decides it is not cost but two normative passages, read in the tree rather than remembered. PROTOCOL.md 3.1: letting a boundary event reach the roster would derive the genesis from *"which boundary events this receiver happened to hear - a per-receiver quantity in the position D-012 forbids above all others"*, and *"that fork is permanent … and no rule in this document can end it"*. PROTOCOL.md 4.10: today a window disagreement reaches canonical state only through HAND_INIT(k+1)'s collective body, where the peers reject each other's copy and stage 0 stalls - *"that is loud … and both peers still hold the same GENESIS(k+1) … so nothing is permanent"*. So the cheap answer, `PLAYER_SIT_IN` as the term that adds to R(k+1), trades a loud recoverable failure for a silent permanent one. **The principle worth keeping from the whole exercise:** the grow side may not introduce a new class of arrival residual, only reuse the one the project has already accepted - and a certificate is admissible where a bare request is not because it exists in as many byte-identical copies as there are healthy seats, because it carries the subject's own signed events so a receiver that never heard the subject can still decide it, and because 3.2 admits a set-enlarging event bound to the chain whose set it enlarges. **And the prerequisite this row did not know it had, found by attacking the recommendation rather than by writing it:** the late-roster repair fires only when `self.aborted().is_some()`, so it is abort-path only, while a return certificate is settled-path only - `checkpoint8` is `None` after an abort and a returning seat has nothing agreed to prove it followed. The two never meet: a late RETURN_CERT would bank, move R(k+1) at whoever received it in time, and notify nobody, and IN(k) lands in `participants`, which 3.1 puts in the permanent class. **So the next thing to build is not the certificate but a settled-path late roster repair** - and reading that prerequisite in the tree on 2026-09-06 re-priced it in both directions. **It is far smaller than this row assumed:** the repair itself is already path-agnostic. `run.rs` takes `late_roster` off the retained hand, calls `next_hand()` and re-opens at the corrected genesis without asking how the hand ended; the only thing welded to the abort path is the single `if self.aborted().is_some()` in `bank`, and `settled()` above it is the admission rule. So the prerequisite is one condition and its tests, not a subsystem. **And it cannot be accepted alone, which this row had backwards.** `settled()` is `Step::Ended`, which is *after* the settled terminal and not after betting, so there is no present event that may legitimately bank late on that path: a `kind = 1` or `kind = 2` `TIMEOUT_CERT` **closes a stage**, and a receiver that reached `HAND_COMPLETE` without chaining that stage has a different `TERMINAL(k)` already - the fork is upstream of the roster, and banking the certificate late repairs nothing. That is exactly what the freeze's own comment says, and it is right. The settled-path repair is therefore admissible only for a **roster-only, non-stage-closing** event, which is the return certificate itself. It can be built first and tested first; it cannot be measured first, and no run will show it doing anything until the event it serves exists. The five corrections, and the first is a trap in this row's own one-line formula: the voter set is R(k) minus OUT(k) **before** the returns are added, or two simultaneous certificates become mutually dependent and receivers applying them in different orders derive different sets; the settled-path condition must be the chain fact *TERMINAL(k) is the HAND_COMPLETE stage hash* and not `checkpoint8.is_some()`, which comes apart on 4.10's abort-settle race; the two-voter floor does not exclude heads-up, because the subject is outside R(k) so removing it removes nothing; `grace` is an unowned gate between the roster growing and the player playing; and a return costs two hands rather than one, with no return at all at an aborted boundary - which is most of them. **Only on the player's own signed request, never automatically:** in a mesh, automatically can only mean *whoever I have heard from lately*, it would silently undo the signed intent of a seat that sat itself out, and the evidence it would use is emitted every hand, so a seat that is only watching would be dealt in against its will. The automation belongs in the client. Cost as re-priced: about 1 650 lines and 700 of tests over eight documents, still major, but the machinery is generalised rather than duplicated. | OPEN: `docs/research/READMISSION.md`, `docs/PROTOCOL.md` 4.9/4.10/8.3, `src/table/hand.rs` (`next_hand`) - designed and corrected, prerequisite named, not built |
| S1-BN | **One lossless message from seat 8 to the founder was re-sent on request thirty-one times over fifty-seven seconds and never arrived; the other seven peers accepted it at once; the founder's own traffic to seat 8 flowed the whole time; and the founder was voted out of its own table for a message that was sent thirty-one times into its router.** Hand #4, `split223028-9`, UTC: seat 8 autoplays Check at 20:34:42.810 (id 528 on the n5->n0 connection). n0's stall tick asks seat 8 for `received_message_id + 1` every two seconds (`patches/0011`, working exactly as built); n5 logs *“Re-sent requested packet 528”* thirty-one times, 227.1 s to 283.2 s. n0's toxcore logs **nothing** — no out-of-order, no receive-array failure, no decrypt error — and n0's status says *“hand waiting for [8], 0 event(s) held”*. At 288.8 s n0 says *“I am at stage 34, 8 last heard at stage 31”* while the other eight say *“stage 35, 0 last heard at 31”*; at 289.8 s they certify seat 0; at 294.3 s n0 says *“this hand has forked”*. **It was the only id in the whole run re-sent ten or more times.** **Where it went, established by reading the C and by four independent traces of the receive, send, address and repair paths.** The receive path has no silent branch — a stale key, a bad size, an unknown peer all log at DEBUG — so the thirty-one copies never reached n0's socket. The send path is UDP-only to `gconn->addr.ip_port` while the peer counts as *direct*, and `gcc_conn_is_direct` is decided by the **inbound** direction alone: n5 kept hearing n0, so n5 kept sending to whatever address it held for n0, `sendto` reported success, and n5 had *“no TCP relays to fall back on”* (its own line at 158 s). **The address it held was replaceable by any ping.** `handle_gc_ping` stores a carried address via `gcc_set_ip_port` **unconditionally and silently** — over an address it is receiving from at that moment — and what a ping carries is `chat->self_ip_port`, which `ipport_self_copy` builds by **preferring a non-LAN echo** from the DHT: behind a NAT that is the router's WAN address. Offered to a peer on the same LAN it is poison; the router does not hairpin. And the sixty-second interval meant to throttle those offers throttled nothing: `last_sent_ip_time` is written once, at peer creation, and never after a send, so past a peer's first minute **every** ping to a not-direct peer carried the address, every twelve seconds. **The recovery is the 58-second peer timeout — logged on n5, inferred on n0.** n5 logs *“Send array stuck; timing out peer”* at 284.6 s, which is 226.8 + `GC_CONFIRMED_PEER_TIMEOUT`; n0's own `peer_timed_out` for n5 would fall at about the same second and **logs nothing**, and neither node logs a handshake between 284 and 330 s. That both sides tore the connection down and rebuilt it as new peers is inferred from one fingerprint — n5's very next re-send is for id **6**, a fresh low id, and n0 then accepts seat 8's hands 5 and 6, which a stream frozen behind a missing 528 could never deliver. **And the rebuild is why the address healed — but only one side's rebuild could, and it was the receiver's.** The group is private, so a timed-out peer goes on a list and `do_timed_out_reconn` sends it a handshake request. n5's own exit could not have reconnected by itself: it re-adds n0 with the very address its thirty-one re-sends had died at, and its first handshake attempt is UDP to that same address. The request that carried traffic was **n0's**: n0's `peer_timed_out` — silent, and tripped after 58 s without any of n5's pings or acks, since the peer-timeout clock is refreshed by lossy packets and fragments and never by an in-order lossless message — put n5 on n0's list, n0 sent a handshake request to n5's still-good address, and at n5 `handle_gc_handshake_request` **overwrote n5's stale address for n0 with the packet's real source** and reset the ids. Whether n5 had already dropped n0 decided only whether it saw that request as a new peer or logged *re-handshaking* (it logged neither, so it had). From then on n5 sends n0 to an address it learned from n0's own packet, which is what let hands 5 and 6 land. In UTC the loss window is **[20:34:41.4, 20:34:42.8]**: n5's Call (id 527) at 41.383 was acknowledged — n0's first request named 528 — and 528 at 42.810 never was. A first reading of this row said today's recovery was gentler than the earlier ones because n0's log had no timeout line; the line is the **sender's**, and it was on n5. **It is not new and it is not rare.** Counting one id re-sent ten or more times as a stall: ten of fourteen runs today had them, one to fifteen a run, and every run with a stall had a peer timeout. The founder's log alone shows five and six stalls in the 11:05 and 12:19 runs, each lasting 54 to 57 seconds. `S1-BK` cut the certificates this causes from 1.52 to 0.33 a run by making the *repair* fast; it could not touch a message that never reaches the socket. **The earlier design panel found this mechanism and this row set it aside.** Its address lens reported, verbatim, *“the address that makes a cross-subnet peer look ‘direct’ is the router's own WAN address, taken from the peer's PING and never validated or cleared”* — and `S1-BK`'s investigation filed it under *not the shape*, because the peers it was then looking at had clean carriers. They did; the carrier is clean in this failure too. That is the whole point of it. **FIXED, `patches/0012`, two hunks, no packet format, type, handshake or ladder touched.** (A) `handle_gc_ping` refuses a carried address while the connection is receiving directly at a different one, and **says so** — *“refused a ping-carried address for a peer we hear directly”* — which is the instrument this fault never had; the poison was silent on both ends. (B) the ping sender does not offer a WAN self address to a peer whose address is on our LAN, and stamps `last_sent_ip_time`, so the interval is an interval. A third hunk — refreshing *direct* on ordinary lossless data — was written, and **withdrawn before it shipped**: a refuter reading the patched tree showed the ordinary lossless path already does that, at `gcc_handle_received_message`; the grep that found the fragment writer had stopped one function short, and the hunk's comment asserted the opposite of the code. **And then the trigger was refuted, and the row has to say so.** The ping-carried address was the working hypothesis for what poisoned n5's view of n0 at 20:34:42, and a refuter excluded it **for that instant, from the code**: n0 received n5's lossless packets directly at 20:34:40.433 and about 20:34:41.5, `gcc_handle_received_message` refreshes `last_received_direct_time` on every direct lossless packet before any other branching, so n0 counted n5 *direct* until at least 20:34:57.5 — and the ping sender carries an address only to a peer it does **not** count direct. The first lost re-send is at 20:34:43.1. For the first fourteen seconds of the fifty-six the mechanism is forbidden by the code it depends on. **So: the two defects are real, `patches/0012` is a guard for a real hazard, and 0012 is not the fix for this run's loss.** What the logs establish is narrower: n5→n0 UDP delivery died between 20:34:41.6 and 20:34:42.8 while n0→n5 stayed alive for the whole window. The cause is **not identified**. Candidates that fit a silent one-way UDP loss between two processes on one Windows host, all open: n0's UDP receive buffer overflowing under eight local instances plus DHT traffic; NAT or firewall connection state; an address change through the handshake path. **The instrument is also weaker than this row first claimed.** Hunk A's refusal line cannot fire within 16 s of a direct packet, so for a fault of this shape it counts nothing. What would decide it next run is two DEBUG lines that do not exist yet: the source `IP:Port` of every UDP packet handled for a peer, compared against `gconn->addr.ip_port`; and every change of a peer's stored address, with old value, new value and caller. That is `patches/0013`, and the refuters extended the list: every change of a stored address with old, new and caller; every packet handled for a peer whose source differs from the address held; each flip of a connection between *direct* and *relayed* and the first blind re-send of a stuck head, with destination and path; transitions of `self_udp_status` and `self_ip_port`; and the two receive-side early returns the enumeration found to be silent. All at DEBUG. Instrumentation, not a fix — and the first run that shows any of them at the moment a pair goes dark is the run that decides this row. **First run with `patches/0013` in, `split235537-9`, 420 s, nine seats:** packets arriving from an address other than the one held **0**, stored-address changes by ping or handshake **4**, direct/relayed flips **64** (one per peer at start is the baseline), first blind re-sends of a stuck head **147**, self-status or self-address changes **11**; stalls **0**, peer timeouts **0**, seats certified **0**, hands **15**. **What those lines said.** The poison happened, live, and to the far seat: at 96 s `far-n0` logged three times *“ping changed a peer's address from 192.168.1.20:3344x to 198.51.100.17:3344x”* — the local LAN's public IP, carried by the pings of three local seats — and `patches/0012`'s refusal line appears **zero** times in nine logs. It could not fire: it refuses only while the peer was heard directly within `GCC_UDP_DIRECT_TIMEOUT` (16 s), and a peer that has been quiet for 16 s is exactly the peer whose address the next ping overwrites. The guard stood aside in the one state that matters. Two more things the instrument showed. Every local node's DHT self-image is the public IP (`… -> 198.51.100.17:port` on all eight), because `ipport_self_copy` prefers the non-LAN echo, so every local ping to a not-direct peer offers the WAN address; hunk B withholds it only when the receiver is held at a LAN address, and on three seats `far-n0` evidently was not. And `far-n0` holds five of the eight local seats at **`172.16.0.1:port` — the far router**, not at 192.168.1.20: the two subnets NAT each other, and all seventy of `far-n0`'s blind re-sends went to those router addresses and were acked in the end. Mixed paths to the same host, then, and the table still played 15 hands with nothing stuck. **`patches/0014`.** The receiver-side mirror of hunk B, with no timer in it: in `handle_gc_ping`, an address that `ip_is_lan` accepts is never replaced by one it does not, and the refusal is logged with both addresses (*“refused a non-LAN address … over a LAN one …”*). A peer we hold on our own LAN cannot be better reached through the public internet, whatever its ping says; the 0012 rule stays behind it for the direct case. The sender now also logs *“offered self address … to a peer held at …”*, so the next run shows both halves of an offer. Wire-neutral like 0012: it changes what a node stores, not what it sends. **First run with `0014` in, `split002630-9`, 420 s, eight seats here and one there, toxcore log on: 23 hands, 23/23 dealt into on every seat, 0 stalls, 0 peer timeouts, 0 certificates, 0 first blind re-sends — the best run of the day by every column.** The refusal fired **24 times, all on `far-n0`, in a 60-second rhythm** (101 s, 161 s, 221 s … 414 s): four local seats — ports 33446, 33448, 33450, 33451 — offered `198.51.100.17:port` and `far-n0` kept `192.168.1.20:port` for each, every minute of the run. The sender's half explains why hunk B never withheld those offers: those four seats logged *“offered self address 198.51.100.17:… to a peer held at (IP invalid…)”* — they hold **no address at all** for `far-n0` and reach it relayed, so `gcc_ip_port_is_set` is false and hunk B, which needs a held LAN address to compare against, cannot apply. The poison was never a direct-timer race; it is the routine `last_sent_ip_time` cadence offering the DHT self-image to a peer the sender cannot reach, and the receiver's rule is the only place it can be stopped. The three other local seats (33447, 33449, 33452) `far-n0` holds at the public IP from the start — learned from the announce, not from a ping — and reached them through the router all run long with nothing stuck, so a public address for a LAN peer is not fatal by itself; what `0012`/`0014` prevent is a *working* LAN address being replaced by one that is not known to work. **`split115952-10` (5+5, 900 s, 2026-09-05 12:00):** the carrier's worst run of the day with every patch in — peer timeouts (*Send array stuck*) `far-n0` 10, `far-n1` 5, `n1` 2, `n2` 1, `n4` 2, spread over the run from 182 s to 844 s; 9 hands, no certificate, no stall of ten re-sends on one packet. The one-way loss between the two boxes is still the open cause; the instruments are in and this run is the next one to read against them. **Where this is written down, and where it is not.** NETWORK_STACK.md 9.8 describes the same-router case for the libp2p path and prescribes mDNS; the carrier-side half of that case is this row and not that section, because S1-AE holds a ratchet - the five specification documents contain no mention of the carrier at all, by test - and a paragraph naming it there tripped the ratchet as it is meant to be tripped. The ratchet is right; the note lives here. **MEASURED, first run with `patches/0012` in, `split232709-9`, 420 s, nine seats:** stalls (an id re-sent ten or more times) **0**, peer timeouts **0**, ping-carried addresses refused over a working direct one **0** — the count that could not exist before — seats certified **0**, hands **21**. One run against a baseline of one to fifteen stalls in ten of fourteen; it is a sample, not a proof, and the refused-address count is the number to watch across the next ones. **Read the zero on that counter correctly:** it means no ping carrying a different address arrived at a directly-heard peer in those 420 s, so hunk A's refusal was never exercised — the run is consistent with the fix and does not demonstrate it. A second 420 s run, `split233931-9`, read the same: 0 / 0 / 0 / 0, twenty hands, all nine seats dealt into all twenty. Hunk B's guard covers same-host seats whichever address they use: `ip_is_lan` returns true for loopback through `ip_is_local` before it tests the private ranges, and the seats here listen on 192.168.1.20 and 127.0.0.1 both. A genuine address change on a directly-heard peer is now accepted only after `GCC_UDP_DIRECT_TIMEOUT` = 16 s of silence from the old one, which is the cost of refusing the poison and is stated here so nobody re-derives it as a regression. | FIXED: `vendor/c-toxcore/toxcore/group_chats.c` (`handle_gc_ping`, the ping sender), `patches/0012`, `tools/build-tox.ps1` |
| S1-BO | **FIXED by `patch 0015`, and it was never as latent as this row said: 44 of the 63 peer timeouts in the day's two bad runs follow a re-handshake on the same node.** An in-place re-handshake resets both message-id counters and the shared key and leaves both rings untouched, so it strands an old-numbered in-flight message, can deliver a stale one under a reused id, and refuses a new one whose slot is still occupied. Found by `S1-BN`'s handshake lens, read line by line and upheld by its refuter, and excluded for `S1-BN` itself on one fact that was true of the nine logs read that day and false of the next two runs: the branch logs *"Handshaked peer ... sent a handshake request; re-handshaking"* unconditionally at DEBUG, and that line appeared in none of those nine. **The lesson is the exclusion, not the mechanism.** This row named its own trigger line and then filed itself as not-firing on the strength of nine logs; two runs later the line was in every one of them. A latent row is only as latent as the last log searched. **The path.** A confirmed peer never re-handshakes on its own — `do_handshakes` skips a handshaked peer — so the trigger is always the *remote* having deleted us and re-added us as a new peer: `do_timed_out_reconn` (private groups, first try at the next 2 s tick, then every 36 s), a sync announce, `load_gc_peers`, or a DHT announce. On the side that receives the request, `handle_gc_handshake_request` recomputes the shared key from the peer's new session key, sets `received_message_id := 1`, and the response sets `send_message_id := 3` (and `send_array_start` with it); the requester's side goes to 2. **Nothing there touches `send_array` or `recv_array`** — the only clearers are `clear_array_entry`, `clear_send_queue_id_range` and `gcc_peer_cleanup` — and `confirmed` stays true, so the application sees neither an exit nor a join. **What that can do.** An in-flight message under the old numbering keeps its slot and its old id in the sender's ring; the resend walk starts from the new counter's slot, so the old entry is not re-sent until the new counter has passed it, and when it is, the receiver — whose expectation was reset small — stores it as far-future or, once the new counter reaches `old_id − 1`, **delivers the stale message as the new stream's next one**. New sends whose `id % 2048` lands on an occupied old slot are refused (`patches/0007`'s *“Failed to create send array entry”*). Packets sealed under the old key that arrive after the swap fail to unwrap, at DEBUG. A `GR_ACK_REQ` for an old id still finds it and re-sends it under the old id. **Measured, 2026-09-05.** The trigger line fires in `split115952-10` on `far-n0` 13 times, `far-n1` 6, `n1` 2, `n2` 1 and `n4` 2 - exactly the five nodes with *Send array stuck* (10, 5, 2, 1 and 2) and no other node; in `split121953-10` on all ten nodes, which is where all ten had timeouts; and in the clean `split094546-10` not once, alongside zero timeouts and zero send-array refusals on every node. **How much of the damage is this row, counted rather than asserted.** Of the 63 timeouts, 44 have a re-handshake before them on the same node and 24 of those are inside one 58-second window of it (median gap 57.6 s in `split115952-10`, 75.6 s in `split121953-10`); the remaining 19 have no re-handshake before them at all, they are the two early bursts between 171 and 192 s, and they stay with `S1-BN`. A gap wider than one window is still this mechanism and not a coincidence - a stale entry only becomes a timeout when the reset counter reaches the slot it sits in - but the honest claim is the correlation, not the arithmetic: the five nodes with the line are the five nodes with the timeout, ten of ten in the second run, and none of ten in the clean one. The chain where it is tight: `far-n1` re-handshake at 406.3 s, refusals from 427.0 s, *Send array stuck* at 459.2 s, the queue draining 2.8 s after that; then 542.4 to 598.2, 664.3 to 719.2, 786.5 to 844.1; `far-n0` 604.2 to 662.7 and 726.0 to 784.3. **The counter that does discriminate is the carrier's own refused-fragment count**, which reached 1406 on `far-n1` and 864 on `far-n0` against 0 on every node of the clean run. *Failed to create send array entry; entry is not empty* does **not** discriminate, and a reader who greps for it will get the wrong answer: the clean run logs 4878 of them and had no timeout at all. **And it does not only lose a message - it stops the seat.** The vendored `gc_send_custom_packet` reports failure unless every reachable peer accepted, and the driver then retains the whole message, so one peer's occupied slot holds a seat's entire outgoing queue until that peer is timed out: 35 to 45 second blocks with the queue climbing to 317 messages, which is what the *transport is behind* lines were saying all along. **One timeout is not this row:** `far-n0`'s four seed timeouts at 181.9 to 183.9 s of `split115952-10` have no re-handshake before them and stay with `S1-BN`'s open one-way loss. **The fix, `patch 0015`.** `gcc_reset_rings` clears every entry of both rings and `last_chunk_id`, and the re-handshake branch calls it and logs the count. Wire-neutral in the strict sense the vendoring rule demands: not one byte changes, and the entries it drops are ones the remote could never have accepted - it reset its own expectation in the same handshake - while the poker layer re-sends the last stages itself. What is owed is a run: the number to beat is 63 *Send array stuck* lines across two runs, and the acceptance is that no such line follows a *re-handshaking* line. **The first run after the patch does not settle it, and the reason is worth more than the run.** `split192124-10` (5+5, 900 s, no fault knobs, the patched binary at both ends and the far copy verified by hash) came back clean: ten of ten seats seated and in the group, 17 to 18 hands each, one genesis per hand, no divergence, no timeout - and **zero *re-handshaking* lines**, so `gcc_reset_rings` never ran once. A clean control, and no evidence about the fix at all. **What the trigger actually needs, now that it has been looked for on purpose: an asymmetric timeout.** The branch fires at node X only when X still holds Y as handshaked while Y has deleted X and re-added it. If both sides time each other out, both come back through `peer_add` with empty rings and nothing is stale. So the precondition is one node ceasing to hear a peer that still hears it - which is `S1-BN`'s one-way loss, the very thing that seeded the cascade in the two bad runs, and which no knob in this tree can produce: `-LinkDown` drops the application's table messages and deliberately leaves the transport alive (`src/net/run.rs`, and its own comment says so), so toxcore's pings keep refreshing `last_received_packet_time` and nobody times anybody out. **The instrument this needs is a one-directional receive drop**: a seat that ignores its peers' packets below the application for longer than `GC_CONFIRMED_PEER_TIMEOUT` while still sending to them. **Built as `patch 0016`**: `P2P_POKER_DEAF_AT/FOR` and the harness's `-DeafAt/-DeafFor/-DeafSeat`, entirely inside `#ifdef P2P_POKER_FAULT_HARNESS`, which `build.rs` defines only under the cargo feature, so a release build has no path to it. Handshake packets are not dropped, only the two kinds that feed the peer timer: a node that could not handshake could never come back, and coming back is the point. The deaf node times its peers out and deletes them, they keep it because they can still hear it, and its handshake requests then land on connections they still hold - which is the branch `patch 0015` clears up after, at every one of them. **The run, `split212753-10` (5+5, 900 s, one local seat deaf for 70 s from 200 s): the fix is accepted.** The instrument did what it was built for - **21 re-handshakes across all ten nodes**, against zero in the run before it - and `gcc_reset_rings` ran and found what the row predicted it would: **3823 stale send entries and 1005 stale recv entries cleared**, which is the mechanism measured rather than argued. **Not one *Send array stuck* line follows a re-handshake anywhere in the run.** The number to beat was 63 such timeouts across two runs with 44 of them following a re-handshake; this run has one timeout in total and it has no re-handshake before it, which leaves it with `S1-BN` where the other nineteen already are. **And the run was not a kind one**, which makes the zero worth more rather than less: with five seats on the four-core far box and a seat deliberately deaf, every node logged long *silent* stretches, the table managed nine hands where a calm run of the same shape manages seventeen, and hand counts ran from three to eight across the nodes. Load makes a timeout more likely, not less. **D-027 was exercised too, and healed.** Hand #6 opened at two genesis values - five nodes with seat 7 still in R, four without - and four nodes then banked the late certificate and re-opened: *"hand #6 re-opens at genesis a254eb6c with seats [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9] (was 45bf6449 …)"*. Every hand of the run ends at one genesis. **One observation is recorded and NOT attributed:** `far-n1` sat in hand #4 from 403.6 s to the end of the run without a terminal, having held a peer's abort twice under `S1-BT`'s new bound. Its own status line at 540 s reads *five* seats silent for ninety seconds at once, so this run cannot tell a bound that failed to release from a node that was isolated; the next run of this instrument was 8+1, where the carrier is calm and a stall is attributable. **`split214929-9` settles it.** Same instrument, one seat on the far box, 900 s: **15 re-handshakes, 5438 stale send entries cleared, and again not one timeout following one**; 48 hands against the loaded run's nine, and 47 of them at a single genesis. The deaf seat did not stall - it fell seven hands behind and **took itself out**, which is the terminus that exists for it: *"this client is out: the table is at hand 7 and this client reached only 4"* at 408.4 s. Its hand #4 is the only genesis that differs anywhere in the run, eight nodes against that one seat, and the seat then stopped dealing. So the bound released everywhere it was asked to, and `far-n1`'s long sit in the loaded run stays what its own status line said it was: a node with five peers silent at once. `vendor **The recv ring is cleared too, and that is the second half of the fix rather than a cost of it.** That ring is the only input to `gcc_recv_pending`, which is the only input to the mid-delivery bit, which is the only thing that holds a timeout vote off a seat that is merely late (`S1-BK`). It looked at first like a protection being taken away from a peer that had just proved itself alive. It is the opposite, and three lines of the vendored source settle it: the same handler resets `received_message_id` to 1 a few statements later, `gcc_check_recv_array` only ever inspects the single slot at `received_message_id + 1`, and `gcc_recv_pending` counts **every** occupied slot. So after a re-handshake exactly one entry is reachable and all the others are dead for good - while the pending count goes on counting them, holding that peer's bit true for the rest of the run and suppressing this node's vote on it at every stage. That is the permanent silence D-026's bound is written against, bought by accident. Clearing the ring removes a lie, not a safeguard; the bit is re-derived from live traffic on the two-second nudge tick, so a peer with anything genuinely in flight sets it again at once. A remedy considered and rejected: latching the bit for a stage budget after a reset would re-create the same false suppression time-boxed, on all ten nodes in a run like `split121953-10`, and would cost a new connection field, an accessor and an FFI entry to do it. **Three more attacks on this patch, and where each ended.** *The clear sat above four `return -1` paths* - connection overflow, an invalid connection, the TCP-out-of-band gate and a public join into a private chat - where the re-handshake never happens and stock loses nothing: upheld, and the call now sits beside `gcc_set_recv_message_id` where the re-handshake is committed, with only a flag set in the branch that notices it. *New sends could land at the old ids in the emptied ring during the three seconds before the response goes out*: refuted in the source, because every `add_to_send_array` is reached through `send_lossless_group_packet`, which returns at its first line while the connection is un-handshaked - and it is un-handshaked for exactly that window. *Clearing the recv ring throws away messages that had arrived and that nobody will send again*: true, and it is the point rather than the cost. `gcc_check_recv_array` picks its entry **by slot index alone** and `process_recv_array_entry` reads the entry's own id only to acknowledge it, so an old entry sitting at the reset index is handed up as the new stream's next message - which is the second harm this row predicted when it was filed, not a delivery worth keeping. | FIXED: `vendor/c-toxcore/toxcore/group_chats.c` (`handle_gc_handshake_request`), `group_connection.c` (`gcc_reset_rings`), `group_connection.h`, `patches/0015-clear-both-rings-on-a-re-handshake.patch`, `tools/build-tox.ps1`, `patches/0016-a-node-that-goes-deaf-to-its-peers.patch` - fixed and accepted |
| S1-BP | **FIXED, the same one word as `S1-BD` on its sibling: `on_late_settlement` returned `DeckDisagrees` before `stage.hear`, and now notes the disagreement and hears the body.** The late path is §4.10's second half — a `HAND_COMPLETE` arriving after this client gave the hand up — and the refusal there did the same thing as in `on_hand_complete` with a worse ending: the late stage never completed, `late.closed` stayed `None`, and `next_hand` fell through to `ABORT_TERMINAL(k)` while the seats that settled kept the settlement's stage hash — the fork the *`HAND_COMPLETE` wins* rule exists to prevent, produced by the check meant to guard it. **Why hearing it cannot move a chip, on either road.** `late` is seeded in two places. `give_up` carries this client's own settlement stage and body across when the deadline expires while settling, so `late.body` is this client's OWN settlement and `late.closed` applies those stacks and no other seat's — `S1-BD`'s argument exactly. When the client gave up before settling, `late.body` is whichever peer spoke first, which is the D-012 question `S1-BD` deferred — and that stage can never complete: `Collective::complete` needs every seat of the required set, this client is in that set, and it never published a `HAND_COMPLETE` for the stage to hear. So a peer-seeded body reaches neither `next_hand` nor `GENESIS(k+1)`. **Test** `a_disagreeing_late_settlement_is_heard_and_the_settlement_wins_over_the_abort`: seat 1 publishes its settlement, its deadline expires before seat 0's copy lands, seat 0's copy differs in `state_hash`; the copy is heard, the report begins *late settlement disagreement with seat 0*, `next_hand` after the copy differs from `next_hand` after the abort, and both seats build hand `k+1` from the same stacks. With the old refusal put back for one run the test fails at the first assertion, so it tests the change and not the scaffolding. **The measurement in between**, over the 45 runs since `S1-BD`'s: the late path closed a stage in none of them (no client gave up with a settlement already published and then heard the rest), so this fix is by reading, as `S1-BD` said its next step would be. And the settlement disagreements `S1-BD` measured at 124 are **0 in every run since `S1-BD`** — all of which were nine seats or fewer; **no ten-seat run has been made since the fix**, so the freeze it made reachable has not yet been exercised where the disagreements occur. What those disagreements are is now known from the four ten-seat 5+5 runs of the evening before `S1-BD` (`split210848-10`, `212806-10`, `215300-10`, `231054-10`: 39 / 82 / 63 / 269 notes, every one in the `state_hash` field alone with every chip agreeing, every run with certificates). In `split231054-10` it is one hand, #17, opened with seats [0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7, 9] after seats 4 and 8 were certified and after a double timeout on seats 6 and 7 in hand #16 whose votes sat at 6/7 for ten seconds; at settlement the table is in **two camps inside `P(k)`** — seats 5 and 7 hold `8362335a`, seats 0, 1, 2, 3, 6, 9 hold `71eeaf4d` — plus the certified-out `far-n1` (seat 8) still computing a settlement of its own (`34209dd9`) and noting all eight. Stacks equal and state hash different means the difference is in a field the note cannot name — `transcript_head` is the likely one, two camps having chained a different event set — and the note prints only two hashes, so the field is not readable from a log. Two things follow and neither is this row: a ten-seat run on the current tree to see the §6.3 freeze fire on exactly this, and a note that carries `transcript_head` and the chained-event count so two nodes' notes can be compared offline. | FIXED: `src/table/hand.rs` (`on_late_settlement`, the test) |
| S1-BQ | **FIXED, in its second shape — the first was refuted before it was committed.** *“This hand has forked: the table acted for seat X at sequence N and played on; this client is at sequence M on a branch of its own, and the two cannot be reconciled”* fired five times in `split003741-10` (5+5, 900 s, the far box carrying five seats on four cores), and every one was a client **behind** the certificate's sequence — 23 vs 27, 0 vs 27, 22 vs 27, 2 vs 45, 37 vs 45 — three of them the subject itself (`far-n3` seat 9, `far-n0` seat 5, `far-n4` seat 8) and two bystanders not dealt in. None was the fork the report describes: D-024 point 4's fork is *two parents at one sequence*, which needs the receiver to have chained something at that sequence, and a receiver behind it has chained nothing there. What `on_timeout_cert` did with such a copy was bank the roster half, print the fork line and return `Ok` — accepted, so never replayed, every voter's copy alike — while `PROTOCOL.md` §5.2.5 (normative) says an event of a stage not yet reached is **held** and re-judged. **The first shape held before `bank_certificate`, and four refuters — code path, protocol, logs, hazards — were run on it before anything was committed. Two refuted it, on one fact.** `bank` is the only writer of `certified` on receipt; `next_hand` derives R(k+1) from `certified` through `took_part`; and in **all five** measured cases the behind client never reached the subject's sequence before its hand ended — each was parked at its own sequence waiting for a *different* seat's event that had been lost (seat 6 at 23, seat 0 at 22, seat 0 at 37, with 6 to 64 later events held), and the abort that ended it was the 30 s stage budget naming that seat, never the certified one. So the held certificate would never have been replayed, `certified` would have stayed empty, R(k+1) would have kept the certified seat, and the client would have opened hand k+1 at a genesis the other nine did not hold — a permanent split where the old code lost only the hand. The old early bank was doing real work: it is why `far-n3` 407.5 s, `far-n0` 659.5 s and `far-n4` 819.9 s printed *certified [9] / [5] / [8]* and all ten clients opened hands #8, #11 and #14 at one genesis each. `bank`'s own comment records the same failure from an earlier day. **The second shape:** `bank_certificate` first, wherever this client stands; then, for a kind-1 certificate whose sequence is ahead of this client's slot, a once-only note (*roster banked, positional half HELD until it is in position*) and `Err(NotYet)`, so the copy is held and replayed like any early event and the certificate stage is built in position on the voters' copies; equal-with-a-different-parent or ahead still reports the fork, which is the only case it ever described. **Tests:** `a_certificate_for_a_stage_not_yet_reached_is_held_and_then_applied` goes through `hold` and `replay_early` as `run.rs` does, asserts the certified seat has left the voter sets while the positional half waits, and ends with seat 0 at the voters' slot; `a_certificate_held_behind_still_narrows_the_next_hand` is the refuters' hazard as a test — the client aborts before reaching the sequence and R(k+1) must still exclude the certified seat. The first passes only with the hold, the second only with bank-first. **What this does not repair, and the refuters were right to insist on it:** the five clients were behind because an earlier event from another seat never arrived — the carrier's loss, `S1-BN`'s open cause — and holding the certificate changes nothing for a client that never reaches its sequence; the held FIFO is 64 deep with no dedup, and three of the five windows were at 64, so the copies would have been evicted anyway; and no run in the corpus shows a held certificate ever replayed and applied — the only field instance of the older voter-set hold (`split105015-10`) was re-held every 2 s for 43 s until the hand ended. The fix removes a false fork report and an unreplayable consumption; it does not make a lagging far seat keep up. **And the first shape was run once before it was replaced, `split084211-10` (5+5, 900 s), and did exactly what the refuters said it would.** In hand #5 the table certified seat 4 (`far-n2`) at sequence 40 while `far-n2` stood at 36; `far-n2` held the copies — the note *HELD until it is in position* every 2 s from 294 s to 350 s, first from seats 2 and 8, then 5, 3, 0 and 9 — never reached 40, ended the hand at 416.7 s on its own stage budget *still waiting for seat(s) [0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9]*, and, with `certified` empty, opened **hand #6 at genesis `fbc02395` with seats [0 … 9]** while the other nine opened it at `9ff4c859` with seat 4 gone: a table of one from then on, 14 hands opened on a branch nobody shared. One run, one client, the predicted split — the second shape's test `a_certificate_held_behind_still_narrows_the_next_hand` fails under the first shape with R(k+1) = [0, 1, 2] for the same reason. The 8+1 run of the first shape (`split083343-9`, 420 s) had no certificate and showed nothing either way: 14 hands, 9/9, 0 stalls, 0 timeouts, first seal 79 s. **The second shape's first run, `split090441-10` (5+5, 900 s): 16 hands, two certificates, and one genesis per hand on every client that opened it — no split.** The held path fired twice, both at the seat-8 certificate (sequence 49): `far-n3`, the subject itself, at 41, and `far-n2`, a bystander at 28, each logging *roster banked, positional half HELD until it is in position* once; neither reached 49 before the hand ended, and both printed *the table changes … certified [8]* at 667–668 s — forty seconds before the in-position seats did — and opened hand #14 at the genesis the other six opened it at. That is the refuters' hazard, closed in the field: the roster half banked first is what a behind client keeps when it never replays. Two more things this run showed. The vote on seat 7 that certified it took 80 s from first vote to roster change (400 s to 480 s) and the vote on seat 8 spread its eight votes over 28 s (638 s to 666 s), the far box carrying five seats on four cores; and hand #8 of the first shape's run, where eight seats waited on one for 370 s with the vote parked at 4/8, did not recur — it is read under its own row. | FIXED: `src/table/hand.rs` (`on_timeout_cert`, the two tests) |

No certificate, so no roster narrowing, so no next hand: a betting stage is deliberately bounded by nothing but the vote and `hand_deadline_ms` (55 minutes for the rated preset), because *a player thinking is legitimate*; the eight would have sat until 07:45. Meanwhile `far-n1` left hand #8 at 546 s on its shuffle-stage budget and derived hands #9 to #14 alone, one abort every 61 s, on the same chain six hands ahead of a table that never left #8. **What separates the four from the four is not the machine.** Three of the silent seats are far and one is local; and the split follows *hearing*: every silent seat's status line had seat 7 fresh (`7 29ms`, `7 245ms` — `far-n1` kept talking, about later hands) and every voter's had it silent (`7 silent 31s`). That points at `S1-BK`'s mid-delivery lever — a seat the carrier is still delivering is *late, not silent* — but that lever yields at `long_past_stage`, twice the budget, and 370 s is seven times it. **Read, and not explained.** Every gate of `vote_on_timeouts` was read against the four: `over()` is `Step::Ended` or aborted, and the status line says neither; `past_stage_deadline` runs on `stage_at_ms`, which `mark_stage` refreshes only when the sequence moved, so re-sent votes cannot hold it (they were suspected); `subject_now` was `Some`, because the silent four counted the received votes and counting needs `same_subject`; `voted` is written only by an own vote; the voter-set gates hold the same set on every client; `say_at` cannot refuse silently — its error is printed as *the clock: …* and no log has that line; receiving a vote writes nothing an own vote reads. Two silent gates were suspected in turn and each refuted by the code before it was named: an accepted vote resetting the stage clock, and a received vote marking the subject as voted. **The instrument.** `Hand::vote_state` — read by the stall tick, said at most every 30 s — names every gate's value on any client whose open stage has outlived its deadline while it has not voted about a seat it waits for: stage age and deadline, the owed type, `long_past_stage`, the subject, the voter set, votes held, the mid-delivery bit. The next 4/8 will say which gate it was. **First run with the instrument, `split092359-10` (5+5, 900 s): 102 lines, and not one unexplained.** Folded by gate: every entry where this client was an eligible voter and had not voted — 171 of them — carries `long past false` **and** `mid-delivery true`: the `S1-BK` lever, holding the vote while the carrier is still delivering that seat's traffic, and never past twice the budget (the longest such wait was 31 s against a 30 s stage). Every `long past true` entry — 7 — is a client that is **not a voter**, a seat certified out earlier and still watching the hand. Seven more had already voted and were waiting on the others. The 370 s silence of hand #8 did not recur; the instrument stays, and the row stays open until a run shows a vote owed, eligible, past twice the budget, and not cast. What the fold does show is how much of a far seat's waiting is the lever at hand start: 133 of the 171 are `HAND_INIT`, the far box carrying five seats on four cores. **Second run, `split094546-10` (5+5, 900 s): 15 hands dealt into all ten seats, 0 certificates, 0 stalls, 0 timeouts, one genesis per hand on all ten; 74 lines, every one either the lever before twice the budget (`ShuffleStep` 43, `DeckInit` 34, `HandInit` 4, `ShuffleProof` 2) or a vote already cast (13); longest wait 31 s against 30.** **Folded over every run on disk on 2026-09-06: still unmet, and the corpus is now five times the size this row records.** Eleven runs carry the instrument and it has printed **581** lines. Every one is accounted for by a category this row already names: **445** are the `S1-BK` lever, a vote held while the carrier is still delivering that seat's traffic; **76** are a client that is not a voter at all — a seat certified out and still watching the hand, which is `S1-BW`'s bystander seen from the vote's side; **60** had already voted and were waiting on the others. **Zero** are the thing this row is open for: a vote owed, eligible, past twice the budget, and not cast. The fold is four lines of Python over the instrument's own line and should be re-run against any future stall rather than re-derived. | OPEN: `src/table/hand.rs` (`vote_on_timeouts`, `vote_state`), `src/net/run.rs` (the stall tick) — open |
