| S1-CA | **FIXED: a join needs two things at once - an advert and a dialable founder - and only the first was ever a trigger, so a client held a valid ticket for a minute at a time and never presented it.** The headless driver asks for a seat when a `TableSeen` event arrives, and that event is emitted only for an advert that gets past the limiter. `Window::admit` is a **tumbling** sixty-second window admitting four adverts per table key, while a founder publishes about ten a minute - a 30 s housekeeping re-advertise plus a re-signed shout to every new lobby subscriber, one per three seconds. So a joiner spends its whole minute in the first eight to ten seconds and is deaf for the rest: `RateLimited` becomes `TableRefused` and no `TableSeen` is emitted **even for a table the store still holds**. Every join failure meanwhile tears the state down (`table = None`, the carrier cleared, the topic unsubscribed), so the client is wholly dependent on that event. **Measured, and reproduced by two readers independently:** holes of 51.1 s (`split111249-10` `n3`, 77.1 to 128.2), 56.5 s (`split083343-9` `far-n0`, 26.2 to 82.7) and twice in one run, 49.9 s and 52.2 s (`split214929-9` `n7`, which was that run's slowest seat). In each of them the founder came up inside the hole and was not asked - `far-n0` logged it at 63.1 s, its next accepted advert at 82.7 s and its seat at **82.9 s**: nineteen and a half seconds holding a ticket it could not present. **The fix is the second edge**, the founder becoming dialable, with a five-second floor and a ceiling of eight asks per run, both in a pure function with a test; the advert edge is untouched and stays unbounded, so nothing that fires today stops firing. **Not a timer, deliberately:** a timer fires while the founder is undialable, which is exactly when the ask is futile - `n7`'s four asks inside one window were each answered *"the founder did not answer"* within a tenth of a second - whereas every seating in the corpus follows its ask by 0.1 to 0.5 s, so the edge fires only where the ask is about to succeed. **One defect in the first shape, found by a refuter.** The five-second floor was armed by an ask from **either** edge, and an advert-edge ask is often dropped before it leaves: the join request is discarded within the same poll when the swarm holds no address for the founder, which is the ordinary case the advert itself creates, and the very same command fires the query that makes the founder dialable a second or two later. So an advert ask armed the floor and silenced the founder edge in exactly the fast case the edge exists for. The floor is the founder edge's own now. **A second defect, from the next reader: the edge fired on the connection the join request itself had dialled.** The node refuses a `JoinTable` while one is in flight, so that ask was spent on nothing at the exact moment the driver was closest to a seat - and 21 of 524 joiner logs record the founder's connection dying with a request in flight, after which the founder stays connected and no further connection event is ever raised for it, which is this row's own hole reintroduced one level down. The driver now tracks its own join, and it is a **window rather than a latch** - the third shape, after I read the node's handler back and found three arms that answer with a `Warning` and `continue` and send no event at all: the table has left the node's own lobby store, the advert's founder id will not parse, the RNG failed. A flag those never clear would silence the founder edge for the life of the process, which is this row's own fault one level down. So an ask is **stamped**, `Seated`, `LeftTable` and `JoinRefused` clear the stamp, and the stamp lapses on its own after `JOIN_RPC_TIMEOUT_MS` + `REASK_FLOOR` = 35 s - the join RPC's own request timeout, past which the node itself calls the request failed, plus the margin between the timeout firing and `LeftTable` arriving. Overrunning it costs one ask answered with a warning, which is what the driver did before this row existed. Both edges of the window are tested. **One limit recorded rather than fixed.** `NodeEvent::PeerConnected` is advisory (`src/net/node.rs:368`), so `Events::send` offers it with `try_send` and drops it when the channel is full rather than waiting - deliberately, because a non-advisory send on a full channel stalls the whole `select!`. The founder edge is therefore a **one-shot trigger on a droppable event**: lose that one event and the driver is back to waiting for an advert. It is left as it is on evidence, not on argument - the node counts every drop and says so, and **none of the 644 logs on disk contains the line**. If one ever does, the answer is not to make the event reliable but to re-derive the condition from state the driver already holds, which is what the ask ceiling and the floor exist to bound. **Two things the readers corrected and the row keeps:** the hole explains the extreme tail rather than the whole bimodality (a run whose adverts were never rate-limited can still take a minute, for `S1-CB`'s reason), and most of the asks the hole delays would have failed anyway on the founder's dial - so this amplifies `S1-CB` rather than standing alone. **The two were meant to land together and did not**: `S1-CB`'s first design was refuted and reverted, so this one lands by itself and must not be expected to move the distribution on its own - the ask it saves still has to survive a dial priced at the full cycle. It is the **headless driver only**: the window a human clicks in is drawn from the store, which outlives the limiter's dead minute. **The run, `split122959-9`, 8+1, 420 s, no fault knobs, and the edge fired twice.** `n6` asked at 59.8 s and `n4` at 61.9 s, and each was **seated 0.1 s and 0.3 s later** - the bottom of the 0.1 to 0.5 s this row's own *not-a-timer* argument was built on, which is the argument checking out. Both had just made an advert-edge ask and lost it: the line immediately above each is *"the founder did not answer: Failed to dial the requested peer"*, which is the dropped-before-it-leaves case the first defect above is about, caught in a run rather than argued from the library. Without this edge both would have waited for the next advert the limiter admitted. The run's last seat was 65.3 s, in the slow group, and that is not this fix failing: the whole cohort was waiting on something upstream of it, and the per-seat measurement is in `S1-CB`. The table then dealt **24 hands, one genesis each, all nine seats agreeing on every one**. | FIXED: `src/main.rs` (`ask_for`, `REASK_FLOOR`, `MAX_CONNECT_ASKS`, `JOIN_ASK_LINGERS`, the founder-connection edge, one test) - **fixed and accepted** |
| S1-CB | **BUILT at the third attempt, measured: a lobby provider whose dial failed waits a whole discovery cycle before it may be tried again, and for the first minute of a run that is the wrong price - the joiner is not waiting for want of information.** `REDIAL_AFTER` is sixty seconds and equals the crawl period, which is right for a record that may simply be dead. But a dial of a **live** peer fails for two reasons that both mend themselves in seconds: the DHT still serves the founder's **previous run's** circuit addresses, because seat identities are stable across runs and the founder's own announcement walk takes about twenty seconds; and the circuit hop collides, because `libp2p-relay` dials the relay with the default `DisconnectedAndNotDialing` condition and drops the pending circuit on **any** dial failure, so a circuit through a relay this client is already dialling dies synchronously as *"oneshot canceled"* - and twenty seconds later that relay is connected and the same address needs no dial at all. **Measured over the 34 split runs on disk, re-derived rather than taken:** seating time splits cleanly on whether every seat reached the founder inside thirty seconds - fifteen runs seat their last seat between 8 and 31 s, the other nineteen between 33 and 210 s, and **nothing lands in between** - with the slow tail clustering on the sixty-second quantum and its multiples. And the joiners hold the information the whole time: a joiner whose first dial missed then saw the founder in later answers a **median of 23 more times**, at most 78, while the cooldown refused to act on any of them. **A fix was built and reverted the same night, and the reversal is the useful half of this row.** It priced the cooldown on reported failures - ten seconds, five times, then the full cycle - and refuters took it apart on four grounds, two of which made it net harmful and one of which was a flooding vector the fix itself introduced. **(1) `DialError::Denied` reached the counter.** The arm above it swallows `Denied` only while the connection budget is below its ceiling, and **637 of the 644 logs on disk reach that ceiling**, at a median of 24.4 s, and 634 of them inside the first 120 s - inside the very window this row is about. `Denied` also has an asynchronous form, raised when a behaviour of **this** process refuses a connection that is already established - this client's own limiter - so the peer is provably alive, and the change charged it for our refusal, re-dialled it six times as often at the moment we were saturated, and then demoted it, the founder among them, for the life of the process. **(2) The counter was charged for dials this site never made:** the join request-response, `dcutr`, `autonat`, both `kad` behaviours and mDNS all dial these peers by id, and dcutr alone spends three dials per `AttemptsExceeded` - one log has 33 of those, about 99 charged failures for peers we hold a circuit to. So the lane silently stopped applying to the relayed and NATed seats that need it most. **(3) The eviction rule the fix introduced is a flooding vector.** Keeping the newest stamps evicts exactly the **demoted** providers, because the fast lane is what keeps a stamp fresh - and each returns with five fast dials again. Provider records are unauthenticated and peer ids are free, so a peer can fill the map with fakes, earn five fast dials per fake, and renew them on every eviction cycle: past the cap the client sits pinned at the redial ceiling instead of decaying, and never recovers. **(4) The evidence offered for that eviction rule was the wrong quantity.** The 598 and 590 counted providers **offered** - `NodeEvent::LobbyPeer` is sent before every filter and before the insert - not providers **dialled**. Replaying the loop the way the code walks it gives a map peak of **346** across the 601 logs the corpus held when it was replayed, against a cap of 512; the old comment's *peak 318* reproduces exactly at `split215815-2/far-n0`, so the sentence being replaced was measuring the right thing. Crossing 512 needs **both** a lobby that offers more than 512 distinct providers **and** ten to twelve minutes of uptime, and no run on disk has both: the 15-minute runs peak at 104-268 because their lobbies are small, and the 600-provider lobbies were observed for 262-400 s. The eviction is therefore unreachable in the corpus - the hazard in (3) has to be forced - and the honest argument for the rule was always the correctness one, never an observed fault. That is a claim this row made after grepping for a number without checking that the grep counted the right thing. **What the refutation points at, and what should be built instead:** the fast lane belongs to **the founder of a held advert**, not to every lobby provider. That is a set of size one rather than 346. It cannot be forced by fake ids, because a fake is not the founder named in an advert this client holds; it makes the eviction question vanish, because one entry is not evicted; and it shrinks (2) rather than dodging it - the join RPC dials the founder by the same behaviour-supplied addresses the crawl uses, so a failure there is the same fact about the same peer, and only `dcutr` and `autonat` remain to be excluded by connection id; and it is the only peer the measurement was ever about. The general fast lane was a bigger mechanism than the fault. **And that design is wrong too, which `split122959-9` showed the same night it was written.** The run timed four things for every seat - when the founder was first **offered** in a lobby answer, when it was first **identified** as a poker peer, when the **advert** arrived, and when the seat sat down. The four fast seats: `n1` 0.6 / 1.2 / 5.1 / 6.2, `n3` ~1 / 2.3 / 3.9 / 5.0, `n5` ~1 / 1.2 / 2.6 / 3.7, `far-n0` 1.7 / 2.2 / 2.3 / 3.5. The four slow ones: `n4` **1.9 / 62.0** / 60.5 / 62.2, `n2` **2.7 / 62.6** / 63.5 / 65.3, `n7` **11.9 / 57.4** / 57.5 / 58.3, `n6` **12.7 / 59.8** / 59.0 / 59.9. Every seat had the founder in a lobby answer inside thirteen seconds; four of them then took **forty-five to sixty more** to connect to it, and each of those was seated 0.3 to 2.7 s after connecting. The gap is one `REDIAL_AFTER`, sitting exactly where this row says it does, and it is now measured per seat rather than inferred from a distribution. **And the joiner held the founder the whole time, counted:** the founder was offered again in a later lobby answer **20, 31, 24 and 27 times** before each of `n4`, `n2`, `n6` and `n7` connected to it, and the cooldown refused every one. Those are not repeats of one record - each is a fresh answer, dialled through whatever addresses `kad` holds at that moment, which is a different attempt each time. **One caveat this row owes itself:** `REDIAL_AFTER` is 60 s and `discover_timer` is 60 s, so *the cooldown refused* and *the next crawl had not yet brought a usable address* are indistinguishable in any run taken with the two equal - all four seats connect at 57-63 s regardless of whether they first saw the founder at 1.9 s or at 12.7 s, which fits the crawl period at least as well as it fits a per-seat cooldown started at discovery. Shortening the one and not the other is what separates them, so the fix is its own experiment and must be read as one. **The advert follows the connection rather than preceding it**, because it travels the lobby mesh - so a fast lane scoped to *the founder of a held advert* **cannot fire**: at two seconds, when it would be needed, this client holds no advert and cannot tell which provider is a founder. The narrowing that made the design safe made it useless, and one run said so before a line of it was written. **The third design, and it is what the measurement actually supports:** keep the lane general - every lobby provider - and bound it **globally** instead of per provider. One budget of fast redials for the whole process, spent oldest-first, cannot be **renewed** by free peer ids: a flooder can spend the budget, once. **Spending it is not free and this row said it was** - a unit is consumed precisely to buy a dial the loop would otherwise have refused, so consuming the budget *is* creating traffic, at most one extra dial per unit and a measured median of 0.51. Replayed over the 642 usable logs, the opening 120 s carry a median **1.67x** the dials they carry today, p90 2.47x, max 3.90x, and the budget drains fully in 334 of 643. What makes it safe is not that it is nothing but that it is **additive and once**, which is the price named below and not a renewable allowance. That disposes of (3) without an eviction rule and of (4) by not depending on the map's size at all, and leaves (1) and (2) as what they always were - which errors count and whose dial they came from, decided at the arm and by `ConnectionId`. **Built, with the budget taken from the corpus rather than guessed.** `FAST_REDIAL_AFTER` = 10 s, `FAST_REDIAL_BUDGET` = 128, `FAST_REDIAL_WINDOW` = 120 s from the node loop; only `Transport`, `WrongPeerId` and `Aborted` count, only this site's own dials count (told apart by the `ConnectionId` its `DialOpts` carries), and the entitlement is spent the moment the provider is re-dialled. **Why 128 and why a window:** replaying every nine- and ten-seat log on disk, the founder's position in the order its providers are first offered is **median 15, p90 99, max 192**, and it is past 128 in **24 of 521** joiner logs - so a budget spent oldest-first reaches the founder in 95% of them, and in the rest the lane is simply shut. The lobbies are far larger than the budget - median **74** providers offered inside the opening two minutes, p90 288, max 454 - which is why the bound is on **rank** and not on the map. **And why a bound at all, rather than just a shorter cooldown:** `REDIALS_PER_ANSWER` is 8 and a 420-second run draws **707 answers**, so a lane every stale record could hold would issue up to 5 656 re-dials where the committed loop issues about 300. The budget is what keeps the worst case additive - a fifth more dials, once, in the opening two minutes. **The run, `split130616-9`, 8+1, 600 s, and it separates the two explanations per seat, which is what this row said it would have to do.** The lane granted **116 second looks** across nine seats, 3 to 20 each, and the budget was never the binding thing: the lowest it fell anywhere was **108 of 128**, so the 120-second window is what bounds it in practice. **Seven of eight joiners identified the founder at 5.7 to 14.9 s and were seated at 6.5 to 18.4 s**, against `split122959-9`'s four seats that took **57.4 to 62.6 s** to reach the same founder on the same shape without the lane. **And the eighth is the honest half.** `n1` had the founder offered at 3.1 s and identified only at **86.6 s**, seated 86.9 s - and its log shows the lane doing exactly what it was built to do and failing anyway: five genuine founder dials at 3.2, 19.7, 33.0, 60.1 and 72.6 s, each one re-tried ten seconds after the last failed, each one answered *"the founder did not answer: Failed to dial the requested peer"*. That is the competing explanation this row wrote down before the run - **the address, not the cooldown** - showing itself in one seat out of eight, and the lane cannot fix it. So what the change does is take the cooldown out of the critical path; what is left is reachability, which belongs to `S1-AA` and to the relay. The table itself: **36 hands, one genesis each**. Two fault knobs were on one seat in this run (`S1-BW`'s), both hand-only and both starting after every seat was seated, so the formation reading is clean. One run cannot move a distribution and this one is not offered as having moved it. **The adversarial round: 23 findings, 6 survived, and every one of the six is now fixed.** Eight of the claims were of the *this floods the network* class - the ceiling measured against the wrong denominator, ghosts draining the budget in the opening seconds, the lane borrowing attempts from the future because `REDIAL_AFTER` restarts from the fast dial - and the verifiers took all eight apart against the corpus, one of them by reproducing the reviewer's own harness and getting different numbers from the same logs. What survived was smaller and sharper. **(1) `DialError::Aborted` is unreachable in this tree, and admitting it was wrong twice over.** It is produced only when `PendingConnection.abort_notifier` is cancelled; the sole taker is `Pool::abort()`, reached only from `Pool::disconnect`, reached only from `Swarm::disconnect_peer_id` and `ToSwarm::CloseConnection` - neither of which exists here, and the one crate in the registry that emits `CloseConnection` is `libp2p-allow-block-list`, which this client does not use. Had it ever fired it would have meant **this client cancelled its own dial**, which is `Denied`'s class and the opposite of the sentence that admitted it. Out of the list, and the test now asserts its absence. **(2) The test's own paragraph is about `Denied` and `Denied` was the one variant it never asserted** - the historically net-harmful case, live inside the window for most of it. Pinned now, with `WrongPeerId` beside it. **(3) The entitlement was spent only when `Swarm::dial` returned `Ok`.** The three refusals it returns **by value** restamped the cooldown without spending the lane, so a provider whose addresses the bogon filter empties would have held its ten-second lane for the rest of the run, long past `FAST_REDIAL_WINDOW` - the window gates only where a lane is *granted*. It is spent with the stamp now, where the stamp's own comment says such decisions belong. **(4) Five new locals were inserted between `dialled_lobby`'s twenty-two line comment and `dialled_lobby` itself**, so the measured history of that map came to sit above `let started`. Moved. **And one census the round corrected:** *601 of 601 logs reach the connection ceiling* is stale - it is **637 of 644** today, at the same median of 24.4 s, with 634 of them inside the 120 s the lane is open. Corrected in all four places it appeared. **(5) And a sentence of this row's own was simply false:** *consuming the budget removes a benefit rather than creating traffic*. A unit is spent precisely to buy a dial the loop would otherwise have refused, so consuming it **is** creating traffic - the corrected form is above, and the verifier reproduced the replay independently over 642 logs and got the same figures to the digit. **(6) And the predicate was named after the wrong question.** It asked whether a failure was *the far end's rather than this client's* - and reconstructing the multi-line `dial failed:` records over all 642 logs, keeping only peers the crawl actually admits, gives 1 552 `Transport` records of which **1 479, 95%, are *"oneshot canceled"*: `libp2p-relay` dropping a pending circuit because this client was already dialling that relay**. Ours, both ends of it. The behaviour was right and the principle was wrong for almost everything it selected - and the principle is what the next reader applies, so *is it the far end's fault* invites putting `Denied` back the day somebody reasons that a limiter refusal proves the peer alive. The question is **transience**: `the_failure_mends_itself` now, with `Denied` excluded because a saturated limiter stays saturated rather than because of blame, and one imprecision left in on purpose - 62% of `Transport` across the corpus is *"Unsupported resolved address"*, which never mends, and is kept out today only by `charge.is_some()`. Telling it apart needs matching a formatted error string, which is a worse dependency than one wasted dial out of 128. | OWED: `src/net/run.rs` (`FAST_REDIAL_AFTER`, `FAST_REDIAL_BUDGET`, `FAST_REDIAL_WINDOW`, `the_failure_mends_itself`, `earns_a_fast_look`, `lobby_dials`, `fast_lane`, two tests) - **built, measured and refuted through** |
| S1-CC | **FIXED: the outage knob was never silent, so every run taken with it measured a seat that was still speaking.** `link_is_down`'s own doc says it drops *"every table message this client would send and every one it would receive"*. Three receive sites and `publish_hand` ask it; the **housekeeping re-send does not**, and that site broadcasts the last `RESEND_STAGES` stages over Tox on every power-of-two tick for as long as there is a hand. A seat with `-LinkDownFor 120` therefore went on emitting its recent stages for the whole outage, and only its *new* stages and its inbound were cut. Found by enumerating the call sites while looking for somewhere to hang `S1-BW`'s missing instrument, not by a run - a run cannot see it, because the knob's own log line says the link is down and the bytes that leave are indistinguishable from a peer's repeat. **What it cost:** `split220636-9`, one of `S1-BW`'s two failed reproductions, used this knob for 120 s on a local seat; that seat was audible throughout, so the run did not test what it was set up to test. Nothing that was *concluded* is retracted - both reproductions failed for reasons the row named and neither turned on the seat's silence - but the instrument is now what its doc always said. **And the second half is the knob `S1-BW` says does not exist**: `P2P_POKER_MUTE_AT` / `_FOR`, a seat that **hears everything and is heard by nobody**. It is the only way to certify a seat out without making it fall behind: every other knob in this tree works by cutting what *reaches* a seat, so by the time the table has voted the seat is hands behind and is a stranger rather than a bystander, which is exactly why both of `S1-BW`'s attempts missed. Muted, a seat misses its decision deadline, is certified out at `k`, and is still level with the table at `k+1` - `split125944-9`'s coincidence, on purpose. Hand traffic only, so a seat can still ratify its way into the table it is going to be thrown out of; one direction, so a receive site keeps asking `link_is_down` alone and the asymmetry is the instrument. Wired into the split harness as `-MuteAt` / `-MuteFor` / `-MuteSeat`; `tools/table-run.ps1` is deliberately not touched, because every run on disk since 2026-09-02 is a `split`. **It works - `split124559-9` certified a seat out at 320.6 s and left it level, which is `S1-BW`'s row - and its first run found a defect in the knob's own clock.** Both fault windows are anchored by a `OnceLock` initialised on the **first call**, and `mouth_is_shut` is called only from the two hand-send sites - so its clock started at this seat's first `publish_hand`, at 50.4 s, and `-MuteAt 180` meant 230 s. Not wrong by a constant either: it moves with how long formation took, so the same arguments mean a different moment in every run, which is the one property a fault instrument may not have. Both clocks are now armed at the top of the node loop, `link_is_down` included - that one anchors itself correctly today only because the arms that call it fire immediately, which is a property of where the calls happen rather than of the knob. **A third instrument defect, caught by reading the header of the run it was about to spoil:** `-DelayCertsSeat` defaults to 0 and the far end's script asks only `$seat -eq $DelayCertsSeat`, so `-DelayCerts` **always** parked the far seat's certificates whether or not anybody meant it - and with the new `-DelayCertsHere` aimed at a local seat the knob was on **two** seats at once, which is two faults in a run set up to measure one. The far end now stands down whenever the knob is aimed here, and the header names the seat that actually has it rather than the parameter's default. The run it would have spoiled was stopped and taken again. | FIXED: `src/net/run.rs` (`mouth_is_shut`, `nothing_leaves`, the re-send site), `tools/table-run-split.ps1` - fixed; `S1-BW`'s acceptance run is what it is for |
| S1-CD | **FIXED, and the row it replaces was wrong in four places - the fault is a receiver bug in this client's own pre-open buffer, not a missing protocol.** Filed as *a seat outside `R` that loses a frame has no way to ask for it*, taken to four independent designers, three judges each, and the panel's first act was to take the row's own evidence apart. **(1) It was not a `HAND_INIT` and not stage 0.** All eight inits arrived; the replay carried the bystander through **stages 0 to 17** and stalled at **18**, `DECK_COMMIT` at eight dealt-in seats, about 101 bytes. **(2) The seat holding it was not unheard.** *"5 silent 47s"* is a **libp2p ping RTT** - `alive` is written only from `Ping` and `ConnectionEstablished` - while a hand rides Tox and nothing else, and the same line reads *"tox 8 seen/8 confirmed/8 wanted, tox friends up 8"*. Every one of the sixteen `not voting` lines also prints `mid-delivery false`, so the carrier held **no undelivered hole** from that seat for 482 seconds. **(3) It was not on a later hand.** It sat in hand #2 until 501.6 s holding that frame in its own `said`. **(4) `said` is not cleared between hands** - the comment that says so points at the wrong mechanism; it is drained and **refilled by kind** at the boundary, keeping the terminal and the checkpoints on purpose. Three of the four designs inherited at least one of these. **What was actually wrong.** The emitter sealed the deck **18.4 s before** the bystander opened the hand, so the pre-open buffer was that frame's only possible route in - and the buffer held exactly **32**, `NEXT_EARLY_CAP` to the unit, while a nine-handed table's run before the first bet is 8 `DECK_INIT` + 8 `SHUFFLE_STEP` + 8 `SHUFFLE_PROOF` + 8 `DECK_COMMIT` + 8 `DEAL_PRIVATE` = **40 frames**. The buffer is structurally eight short, and its admission was a **match guard**: full meant `_ => {}`, so every later arrival was dropped - and the last to arrive at a WAN peer is exactly the frame it turned out to need. `Hand::early` then evicted by **arrival** (`pop_front`), which for a client walking stages upward destroys the contiguous run immediately above its cursor; that queue sat saturated at 64 from 510 s to the end of the run. **Both queues now evict the highest sequence and drop an arrival that is itself the highest**, because a client that is behind needs the low sequences first and the high ones are still covered by their emitters' ordinary re-send. Four tests, and they are worth the trouble: the old decision was a match guard inside the node loop, where no unit test could reach it. **What the panel recommends next, and why it is not built yet.** A **wake** - a seat inside `R` repeating its own small stored frames of the running hand, unasked, three times a hand, gated on `seats \ required` being non-empty, which is a function of the signed opening at every peer and is empty at every healthy table. It scored highest of the four but the panel's own instruction is to **land the eviction repair alone first**: if the bystander then completes the hand, `S1-CD` as measured was a receiver bug and the wake is insurance against a hazard this corpus has not observed. **Three things the judges killed, so nobody re-proposes them:** filtering `said` on the running hand id (it silences the terminal and checkpoint repeats the boundary drain keeps **on purpose**); a `said` retention across hands (built on the false premise in (4)); and **terminal adoption** by a bystander, which is a verified break - `on_late_settlement` does not enforce unanimity, so adoption would put `roster_hash(k+1)` and hence `GENESIS(k+1)` on *which copy arrived first*, which is `D-012`'s forbidden position verbatim. **And the cheap end is ruled out in writing:** a pull already exists at the carrier - the stall tick nudges every seat in `waiting_for`, which becomes `gc_send_message_ack(..., GR_ACK_REQ)` and repairs a hole in the lossless ring. It cannot ask for a frame the carrier already delivered, and `mid-delivery false` says there was no hole. So `S1-BZ`'s price for a new wire type buys nothing an emitter cannot volunteer. **The run came, and it moves the stall by exactly one stage and does not close it.** `split153802-9`, the same recipe: a seat certified out at 368.9 s opened hand #3 at 387.8 s - 17.5 s after the founder - carrying *"8 init(s) and 32 early frame(s)"* again, and stalled at **stage 19, `DEAL_PRIVATE`**, where before the repair it stalled at **stage 18, `DECK_COMMIT`**. One stage further, and then the same shape: `early` saturated at 64, no further hand opened, the table four hands ahead by the end. **So the eviction rule was right and the panel's *"eviction, not size, is the fix"* was wrong, and its own arithmetic said so:** a nine-handed table's run before the first bet is **40 frames** and the buffer holds **32**, so keeping the lowest 32 buys exactly the stages those 32 cover and stalls at the 33rd. That is what a one-stage gain looks like. The size question is now measured rather than argued, and it is not a free parameter: `SHUFFLE_PROOF` is 16 KB, so a cap of 48 is three quarters of a megabyte at a boundary. **The panel answered it, and its first act was to find what nobody was looking for: every slot in that buffer is a SIXTEEN-KILOBYTE slot.** The only length limit a frame passes on its way in is `FRAME_CAP` = 16 384, applied to the whole frame by `from_canonical` inside `open_inner`; `check_envelope` never reads the payload's length, and the per-type caps are applied at **replay** by `chained::payload`. So a `DECK_COMMIT` whose own cap is 160 bytes could occupy 16 384 for a whole boundary and be discarded minutes later, and this row's worry - *"`SHUFFLE_PROOF` is 16 KB, so a cap of 48 is three quarters of a megabyte"* - was arithmetically right and causally wrong: **thirty-two slots already cost 524 288 bytes.** The expense was the missing ceiling at the door, not the frame count. **So the unit changes.** `frame_ceiling(kind)` charges every frame its own payload cap plus `ENVELOPE_MAX` = 384 - the specification's own §9.2 allowance, against a counted worst case of 214, chosen with its slack because a gate that refuses a **legal** frame is indistinguishable in a log from the loss this row exists to end - as an exhaustive match with no wildcard, so a type added later must be classified rather than admitted at `FRAME_CAP`. Five types with no published cap fall back to `FRAME_CAP` and the doc says which and why. The buffer is then bounded in **bytes**, derived rather than written: `PRE_BET_BYTES_PER_SEAT` = 18 252 from the five frames that stand between a hand opening and its first bet, `NEXT_EARLY_BYTES_PER_SEAT` = 26 956 with one spare maximal frame, `NEXT_EARLY_BYTES` = `MAX_SEATS` x that = 269 560. **The part and the whole are one rule counted twice, so no seat can ever be evicted on another's behalf.** The frame counts survive as slot bounds and go up: `NEXT_EARLY_PER_SEAT` 6 -> **8**, `NEXT_EARLY_CAP` 32 -> **80**, derived as `MAX_SEATS` x the share, with a `const` assertion pinning the drain invariant `NEXT_INITS_CAP + NEXT_EARLY_CAP <= EARLY_CAP` that until now held by luck. `Hand::early` takes the same ceiling and `EARLY_BYTES`, because a retained previous hand keeps a second one alive at exactly the boundary and the client pays twice. **Summed across every buffer at a boundary, worst case, attacker-chosen: 3.73 MiB before, 1.39 MiB after - a 2.7x reduction while the buffer's capacity goes 32 -> 80 frames.** And the open line now reports what was **lost**: *"(N refused, M evicted)"*. That is the falsifier this row lacked - a bystander that still stalls while the line reads zero and zero proves the frame never arrived and the buffer was never the fault, which is the fork between this design and the panel's standing recommendation, the wake. **The run, `split165440-9`, and the counters earned their keep immediately.** The bystander opened hand #3 holding *"8 init(s) and 45 early frame(s) ... (0 refused, 0 evicted)"* — up from the 32 that were `NEXT_EARLY_CAP` exactly, with **nothing lost**, which is the budget doing precisely what it was sized for and the panel's count of 46 confirmed to within one frame. It did not complete the hand, and the counters are what make that informative rather than another ambiguous stall: **zero refused and zero evicted says the buffer was not the fault this time.** What stopped it was a different thing entirely, filed as `S1-CE`, and this run does not repeat the earlier recipe cleanly enough to settle whether the budget closes the stage-19 stall — the table melted down around it, certifying six seats out inside one second. Six tests, including the two that matter: a frame larger than its own type allows is refused at the door and counted, and the bytes bind before the slots when the frames are large. **And a seventh that is the one standing between this gate and a silent liveness regression:** a payload-maximal frame of every type the gate sees — the whole pre-bet run, the actions, and the four roster-bearing types `hold` also gates, `TIMEOUT_VOTE`, `TIMEOUT_CERT`, `HAND_COMPLETE` and `STATE_HASH` — sealed with a maximal `hand_id`, `sequence` and clock, must fit its own ceiling. A `const` assertion cannot see this, because the envelope's size is a property of the encoder rather than of the constants; and the cost of being wrong is not a dropped frame but a **`Malformed`**, which the node turns into a gossip `Reject` and so stops forwarding as well. It is also what exonerated this change when the very next run forked: the ceiling cannot refuse a legal certificate. **Killed by the judges, so nobody re-proposes them:** excluding the shuffle chain (a client missing one link can never verify `DECK_COMMIT`, so it would stall at stage **2** instead of 19 - and the measured run shows the chain being consumed); a larger frame count alone (48 slots at `FRAME_CAP` are 786 432 bytes, worse than today); a byte budget alone (the eviction scan is two CBOR decodes per held entry and 26 956 bytes of `ACTION_*` frames is sixty slots); and deriving the cap from the **live** seat count, which `DEAL_PRIVATE_CAP`'s own comment already refuses in writing and which is unknowable when a frame of `k+1` arrives at a client still in `k`. | OPEN: `src/net/run.rs` (`keep_early`, four tests), `src/table/hand.rs` (`EARLY_CAP`, `hold`'s eviction) — **fixed and it was not enough**: the eviction was right and the size is now the question, and it goes back to the panel with this run rather than being guessed at |
| S1-CE | **OPEN, diagnosed: a lagging client completes a certificate the table never completes, shrinks its own roster below the table's, and can never come back — because rosters only shrink.** `split165440-9`, hand #3: eight clients opened it between 343.7 s and 351.2 s at genesis `8217a558` with `[0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8]`; the ninth — the bystander, certified out at 348.4 s and therefore still inside hand #2 — opened it at 368.1 s at genesis `b020afa1` with `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8]`. **The mechanism, and the tree logs every step of it.** Only that client ever certified seat 0's timeout: it was the only one still in hand #2 long enough to complete the round, and it completed it with **itself** as a voter — *"unanimously among [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8]"*, where 6 is the bystander, legitimate because it was still in `R(2)`. The other eight received that certificate during hand #3 and refused it correctly, each printing *"a certificate about hand #2 from seat Some(6) arrived during hand #3: hand #2 is no longer held here, so it cannot repair anything"*. So `D-027`'s repair fired at exactly one client. **And the trap is `D-024`.** `next_hand` filters `self.open.required`, so `R(k+1) ⊆ R(k)` always — the client's roster is now a **strict subset** of the table's, and no certificate can move it back, because a certificate only ever removes. It did not shrink too late; it shrank **too far**, and monotonicity forbids the return. That is `S1-BM`'s return certificate seen from a new direction and it is the only shape of repair that could work. **This row's first draft said nothing stalls to say so, and that was wrong — the client says so, twice, in its own words.** At 368.1 s: *"hand #3: two seats or more signed it at another genesis before this client opened it, and none at this one — this client's own `HAND_INIT` is withheld until the table is counted here or a certificate re-derives the hand"*, so `quiet_if_contested` did fire and it opened **Quiet**. At 369.2 s: *"hand #3: seat(s) [3, 8] opened it at genesis 8217a558; this client opened it at b020afa1 and nobody has been heard at that genesis."* **The detection is right and complete; what is missing is the road back.** And the sentence that line ends on — *"A certificate about hand #2 would repair it"* — **is false in this direction, and it is fixed here.** Repair would have to *add* seat 0, and nothing in this protocol adds. **The evidence was in the client's hands and thrown away one line earlier:** a seat outside its own `required` that signs this hand is filtered out of `foreign_genesis` on the ground that *"a seat certified out of this hand opens the hand it thinks it is in, and its word does not contest this one"* — right when the table removed the seat too, and exactly wrong when **this client** removed one the table kept. That is why the note named seats `[3, 8]` and not seat 0, whose `HAND_INIT` at the table's own genesis was the proof. Those seats are now kept in `foreign_outside`, and when any exist the line reads *"seat(s) [0] are at that genesis and this client has removed them, so its roster is a strict subset of the table's and NO certificate can repair it: a certificate only removes"*. It changes no decision — the client was already `Quiet` — but it is the difference between a client that is waiting and a client that knows it is stranded, and it is what the next run will print instead of a falsehood. **Nor does it ever end.** The client sat on hand #3 for the remaining 530 s while the table reached #9, and `note_a_hand_ahead` never fired — its only acting reader is the next-hand timer arm, reached once the running hand is over, which for a client stuck mid-hand is never. That gap is already recorded in `S1-BW` and `S1-CD`; here it is what turns a detected fork into a permanent one. **What is owed:** a decision on whether a client outside `R(k+1)` may complete a certificate round about hand `k` at all once the table has left it — which is the cheap end — or whether this waits for `S1-BM`. Not caused by the buffer work landed the same hour: the sealing test in `S1-CD` pins that the new ceiling cannot refuse a legal frame of any roster-bearing type. | OPEN: `src/table/hand.rs` (`bank`, `next_hand`, `vote_on_timeouts`, `quiet_if_contested`'s message), `src/net/run.rs` (the late-certificate arm, `note_a_hand_ahead`'s only reader), `docs/PROTOCOL.md` 4.10, `D-024`, `D-027` — **diagnosed; the false line is fixed, the fork is not** |

**Settled and removed from this list:** the `DISPUTE` self-equivocation question
(review N4) is answered by D-009 rule 1 — the slot key must include every field
that legitimately varies — and is no longer open.

### What is waiting on the project owner, as of 2026-09-02

**Every other `S1` row is either closed or is work that can proceed without a
decision. These five cannot**, and they are gathered here because a decision
spread across five long table rows is a decision nobody can see the shape of.
The rows themselves stay normative; this is an index, not a second owner
(D-011 rule 1).

| # | The decision | Why it is not an implementation's | Cost of getting it wrong |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **`seed → button`, and `seed → seat permutation` in the same breath** (`S1-B`, `S1-AD`) | It decides `HAND_INIT n(1)`, which every peer recomputes as validation, so a rule chosen in one implementation is a wire value invented. §4.4 says the rule is `STATE_MACHINE.md`'s, §7.9 says the constructions are §4.4's and that the engine computes neither — a citation cycle with nothing at the centre. | Until it exists the initial button is grindable by whoever ratifies last, at **11 hashes worst case at six seats and 22 at ten** — microseconds. Worth about one hand of position plus ~1 % of a stack at the first blind boundary, so it is open rather than urgent. `S1-AD` must be answered with it: §4.3 lets a joiner name its seat and §4.4 says the seed decides the seating, and the two collide the moment the beacon exists. |
| 2 | **Write D-019's four Tox fields into §4.3 and §7.2** (`S1-AE`) | It is a wire definition. By §10.2's own rule, *any* change to a payload struct's field set is a major-version change; `grep -ci tox` over all five specification documents returns **zero, five times**. | A conforming second implementation **cannot complete formation** with this client: the fields sit in `JOIN_REQUEST`, `JOIN_ACCEPT`, `PLAYER_LIST` and `LOBBY_TABLE_AD`, and §10.2 says there is no ignore-unknown-trailing-fields behaviour *and there cannot be one*. **Free before release; a major bump after.** |
| 3 | **Correct `PROTOCOL.md` §1.4 and `NETWORK_STACK.md` §8 for D-019** (`S1-A`) | D-019 already decides it — a numbered decision beats a specification — but saying *where the traffic actually is* means ratifying the same invented values as row 2, plus `table::fragment`'s 8-byte header and the per-table GossipSub topic string. | The documents route groups 3–8 to a table mesh that has no implementation and should not get one: it would be `n(n-1)/2` circuits — 45 at ten seats — each under the 128 KiB relay cap D-019 exists to escape. |
| 4 | **The three passages quoting `SPEC_CS.md` verbatim** (`S1-E`, at §1.2, §2 and §9.1) | `SPEC_CS.md` §1 and §3 mandate Mainline and outrank `NETWORK_STACK.md`. Correcting them in place would put the document in front of its own authority; amending `SPEC_CS.md` is the owner's. | The rest of `NETWORK_STACK.md` is now current and these three are not, so the file contradicts itself where a reader is most likely to trust it. |
| 5 | **`Q-10`: does a peer's own emission count into its own participation record?** (`S1-R`, `S1-V`, and `S1-O`'s last sentence) | The corpus asks it of itself and says no construction can close it — agreeing who contributed to the stage that stalled needs a collective step at exactly the point collectivity failed. | It is the last thing between `R(k+1)` and a definition. `S1-V`'s stated blocker turned out to be false — `state_hash` reads `signed` directly, so changing `participants()` moves `P(k)` and leaves §6.1 byte-identical — so what is left really is only this. **Answered on 2026-09-05, and the answer is `docs/research/READMISSION.md`:** a certificate-gated grow side, symmetric with the timeout certificate, because PROTOCOL.md 3.1 and 4.10 twice forbid the cheap answer of letting a boundary event reach the genesis - it would trade a loud recoverable stall for a silent permanent fork. What is now waiting on the owner is not the shape but the order: the design has a prerequisite it did not know it had, because the late roster repair fires only on an aborted hand while a return certificate can exist only on a settled one, so the next thing to build is that repair rather than the certificate. |

**Rows 1 and 2 are the two that a second implementation trips over**, and they
are of different kinds: row 2 makes two clients **meet and fail**, which is
worse than row 1's silent unfairness and cheaper to fix. Row 2 is also the only
one of the five whose cost rises on a date rather than on a decision.
